     service do? (PLAN.md A4) (Auto-compensate (Recommended) / Explicit state 
     only / Auto-compensate, only if time)

     journal + resume (Recommended) / Journal on disk, no auto-resume / Pure 
     in-memory)

● Sure — what would you like to clarify before I re-ask?

  For reference, the four were:

  1. D1 — durable runtime. Hand-rolled journal engine / Temporal /
  Inngest-Restate / Vercel Workflows.
  2. A1 — API shape. POST /bookings fully sync, sync-with-deadline (202 pending
  fallback), or fully async + poll.
  3. A4 — compensation. Auto-refund when charged-but-not-booked, explicit
  terminal state only, or explicit-state-first-then-refund-if-time.
  4. A8 — durability. Journal + resume on boot, journal on disk without resume,
  or pure in-memory.

  Happy to expand the reasoning on any of them, add options I missed, or split
  one into finer-grained questions — tell me which, and anything you already
  have a view on.

      355  
      356 +### D1.1 — Consequences of choosing WDK (constraints discovere
          +d during research)                                            
      357 +                                                              
      358 +Three findings materially shape the design. All three are docu
          +mented behaviour, not bugs,                                   
      359 +and each is worth naming explicitly in `README.md` / `PROCESS.
          +md`.                                                          
      360 +                                                              
      361 +**C1 — `start()` accepts no idempotency key, so WDK cannot sat
          +isfy M7 on its own.**                                         
      362 +                                                              
      363 +`start(workflowFn, args)` returns a `Run` with a generated `ru
          +nId`. There is no                                             
      364 +caller-supplied run id and no keyed/atomic start. WDK's own do
          +cumented run-idempotency                                      
      365 +pattern is *post-hoc*: both concurrent callers may call `start
          +()`, and the losing run                                       
      366 +detects the collision inside the workflow body via `hook.getCo
          +nflict()` and returns early                                   
      367 +as a duplicate. The framework's docs state the race plainly: *
          +"Two concurrent requests can                                  
      368 +both observe 'no hook yet' and each call `start()`."*         
      369 +                                                              
      370 +That is a legitimate design (the duplicate does no work), but 
          +it does **not** satisfy M7                                    
      371 +as written: the assignment requires that two simultaneous POST
          +s "trigger exactly one                                        
      372 +workflow run" and that we *verify the workflow-run count*. Und
          +er the native pattern the                                     
      373 +count is two.                                                 
      374 +                                                              
      375 +**Resolution:** idempotency is claimed at the **API layer, bef
          +ore `start()` is called**, by                                 
      376 +an atomic claim on the `Idempotency-Key` in our own store. Nod
          +e's single-threaded event                                     
      377 +loop makes a correct atomic claim genuinely easy here: the che
          +ck-and-insert is synchronous,                                 
      378 +and the loser awaits the winner's in-flight promise rather tha
          +n starting anything. WDK's                                    
      379 +hook-conflict pattern is then a redundant second line of defen
          +ce we may adopt if cheap —                                    
      380 +but the claim is what makes the run count one.                
      381 +                                                              
      382 +A second reason we cannot lean on hooks for this: hook tokens 
          +are only unique *while                                        
      383 +active*, and are released when the run completes. Idempotent r
          +eplay (A3) must work after                                    
      384 +the booking has finished, so the idempotency record has to out
          +live the run regardless.                                      
      385 +                                                              
      386 +**C2 — There is no documented programmatic API for reading run
          +/step history.**                                              
      387 +                                                              
      388 +Observability is exposed through the CLI (`npx workflow inspec
          +t runs`,                                                      
      389 +`npx workflow inspect run <id>`) and a web UI (`npx workflow w
          +eb`). `getRun(runId)`                                         
      390 +returns status, `returnValue`, and a readable stream — not an 
          +enumerable history of steps,                                  
      391 +attempts, and timings. *(Caveat: this is the state of the docs
          + we could reach; if an API                                    
      392 +exists, prefer it and simplify accordingly. Verify at build ti
          +me before writing our own.)*                                  
      393 +                                                              
      394 +**Resolution:** the timeline is a projection of **our own** bo
          +oking record, not of WDK's                                    
      395 +journal. This still honours the "no separate audit table" cons
          +traint, because the                                           
      396 +artifacts it projects are ones correctness already forces us t
          +o store: the request                                          
      397 +fingerprint (A3), every provider call's idempotency key and ou
          +tcome (needed for replay and                                  
      398 +to decide compensation), and each state transition. The timeli
          +ne additionally carries the                                   
      399 +WDK `runId`, so an investigator can cross-reference `npx workf
          +low inspect run <runId>` for                                  
      400 +the framework's own view — which is a small, honest version of
          + the reconciliation idea in                                   
      401 +N3.                                                           
      402 +                                                              
      403 +**C3 — The Local World does not resume interrupted runs across
          + a restart.**                                                 
      404 +                                                              
      405 +Run data is written to `.workflow-data/`, but the local queue 
          +is in-memory: per the docs,                                   
      406 +"steps are queued in memory and do not persist across server r
          +estarts," and interrupted                                     
      407 +runs do not resume. Durable resumption is a property of the Ve
          +rcel World (or a Postgres                                     
      408 +World), not of local development.                             
      409 +                                                              
      410 +**Resolution:** this settles A8 — we do **not** attempt to dem
          +onstrate restart recovery,                                    
      411 +because in the configuration a reviewer will actually run, it 
          +does not exist. We say so                                     
      412 +plainly in the README, name the World that would provide it, a
          +nd spend the saved budget on                                  
      413 +the failure matrix and the timeline. Claiming crash-resumption
          + we cannot demonstrate would                                  
      414 +be exactly the kind of unverified assertion the assignment ask
          +s us to avoid.                                                
      415 +                                                              
      416 +### D2 — HTTP framework and test runner `OPEN` (narrowed)     
      417 +                                                              
      418 +TypeScript is given. WDK requires a framework integration, whi
          +ch rules out bare                                             
      419 +`node:http`. Shortlist: **Hono** (smallest, fast, first-class 
          +WDK integration, trivial to                                   
      420 +drive in tests via `app.request()`) or **Express** (most famil
          +iar to a reviewer). Bias:                                     
      421 +Hono, for testability without binding a port.                 
      422 +                                                              
      423 +Test runner: **Vitest**, for async ergonomics, fake timers, an
          +d concurrent-test control.                                    
      424 +Confirm WDK's build plugin composes with the chosen runner ear
          +ly — steps are compiled into                                  
      425 +isolated routes by a bundler plugin, and the testing story was
          + not documented in the                                        
      426 +material we could reach. **This is the single biggest schedule
          + risk in the plan; spike it                                   
      427 +in the first 15 minutes** (see §11 when written) before buildi
          +ng anything on top.                                           
      428 +

      319  Retries plus real sleeps make a test suite slow and flaky, and
            wall-clock deadlines
      320  (A1c) make it worse.
      321  
      322 -**Resolution:** all delay goes through an injectable clock/sle
          -ep. Tests use a fake clock                                    
      323 -and assert on the *sequence* of attempts, not elapsed time. Re
          -try policy is per-step                                        
      324 -data (max attempts, backoff, which error classes are retryable
          -), declared next to the                                       
      325 -step, and visible in the timeline.                            
      322 +**Resolution:** retry policy is per-step data declared next to
          + the step, using WDK's own                                    
      323 +vocabulary rather than a homegrown one:                       
      324  
      325 +- `stepFn.maxRetries = n` — the attempt budget for that step. 
      326 +- `FatalError` — thrown for errors that must **not** be retrie
          +d (validation rejects, a                                      
      327 +  provider's permanent decline). This is how a step says "retr
          +ying this changes nothing".                                   
      328 +- `RetryableError(msg, { retryAfter })` — thrown for transient
          + failures, with the backoff                                   
      329 +  the step itself chooses; `getStepMetadata().attempt` gives e
          +xponential backoff.                                           
      330 +                                                              
      331 +Tests assert on the *sequence and count of attempts*, never el
          +apsed wall-clock time. Keep                                   
      332 +`retryAfter` values small in test configuration so the suite s
          +tays fast without needing to                                  
      333 +fake the framework's internal clock, which we do not control. 
          +Every attempt and its                                         
      334 +classification is recorded in our timeline (A12).             
      335 +

  Listed 1 directory, wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

● Your assumption was correct. Local World is a real thing, it's bundled with
  workflow, needs zero config, and stores run data as JSON under
  .workflow-data/. WDK also integrates with Hono/Express/Fastify — no Next.js
  required for a two-endpoint service. Same workflow code deploys to the Vercel
  World by switching WORKFLOW_TARGET_WORLD. So yes: proper workflow locally,
  deployable unchanged. I ranked it last from priors, not evidence — that was
  wrong, and it's now D1: DECIDED — WDK.

  Also in WDK's favour, which I hadn't credited: it gives us the exact retry
  vocabulary grading criterion #2 asks about (maxRetries, FatalError,
  RetryableError({ retryAfter }), getStepMetadata().attempt), documents the
  saga/compensation pattern we need for A4, and has native hooks for the
  webhook-resume nice-to-have.

  Three constraints the research turned up (now PLAN.md §D1.1):

  C1 — start() takes no idempotency key, so WDK alone cannot satisfy M7. Their
  documented dedup is post-hoc: both concurrent callers may call start(), and
  the loser detects the collision inside the workflow via hook.getConflict() and
  exits early. Their docs say it outright — "Two concurrent requests can both 
  observe 'no hook yet' and each call start()." That's a defensible design, but
  the assignment requires exactly one run verified by run count, and the native
  pattern counts two. So we claim the Idempotency-Key atomically at the API
  layer before start(). Node's single-threaded loop makes that genuinely easy.
  This is probably your strongest PROCESS.md entry — the framework's own answer
  doesn't meet the spec, and you can say precisely why.

  (Secondary reason we can't lean on hooks: hook tokens are only unique while 
  active and are released on completion. Idempotent replay must work after the
  booking finishes, so the record has to outlive the run anyway.)

  C2 — no documented programmatic API for reading run/step history.
  Observability is CLI (npx workflow inspect run <id>) and web UI. getRun()
  gives status/returnValue/stream, not an enumerable step-and-attempt history.
  So the timeline projects our records — which still satisfies "no separate
  audit table", since correctness already forces us to store the request
  fingerprint, every provider call's key and outcome, and each state transition.
  We carry the runId so a reviewer can cross-check the framework's view.
  Caveat: that's from the docs I could reach; worth 5 minutes verifying at build
  time before writing our own.

  C3 — Local World does not resume interrupted runs. Run data hits disk, but the
  local queue is in-memory: "steps are queued in memory and do not persist 
  across server restarts." Resumption belongs to the Vercel/Postgres World. This
  closes A8 — we don't demo restart recovery, we state plainly which property
  belongs to which World. Timelines survive a restart even though in-flight runs
  don't, which is the half that matters at 2am.

  One schedule risk: WDK compiles steps into isolated routes via a bundler
  plugin, and I found no testing documentation. Since the whole submission rests
  on the test suite, that wants a 15-minute spike — Vitest + Hono + one trivial
  workflow, asserting a run count — before anything is built on top. Flagged in
  D2.

  Still open: A1 (sync / sync-with-deadline / async — now a pure API-design
  call, since run.returnValue makes all three trivial), A4 (auto-compensate vs
  explicit terminal state), A12 (timeline format), D2 (Hono vs Express). Want to
  take those next, or spike the WDK+Vitest setup first so we're planning
  against verified ground?

  Sources: Introducing WDK · Local World · Run idempotency · Starting workflows
  · Errors and retries · Observability · vercel/workflow

❯ Ok I will review the larger plan, but first, in C1 you are relying on single

  locally. Or rely on Idempotency-Key and the APIs we use without restricting

● This is the right objection, and it's load-bearing. "Node is single-threaded"
  isn't a correctness argument — it's an argument that happens to hold in
  exactly one deployment topology, and the topology we ship to isn't it. Let me
  answer directly, then write it up.

  What's best: neither lock nor provider-keys-only. Both, as layers with 
  distinct guarantees.

  Your second option — rely on provider idempotency and permit multiple runs —
  is necessary but not sufficient. It bounds the money damage perfectly:
  deterministic keys mean N concurrent runs still produce one hold and one
  charge. What it doesn't bound is decision damage. Two runs that both reach
  compensation can have run A refund while run B consumes the hold. Idempotent
  providers can't save you there, because those are two different,
  individually-legitimate calls.

  A Redis lock is also the wrong primitive: leases need TTLs, TTLs need fencing
  tokens, and a lock expiring mid-workflow is a new failure mode. What we
  actually want is a durable conditional write — a unique constraint, not mutual
  exclusion. SET NX / INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING / DynamoDB
  attribute_not_exists. One round trip, no expiry semantics in the correctness
  path, and the record has to outlive the run anyway for post-completion replay.

● CORRECTNESS.md now exists with §1–§4 written; §5–§7 (happy path, failure
  taxonomy, test matrix) are stubbed for next.

  Your premise is now §1, stated as a hard two-part standard: under-5-minute
  clean clone, and no correctness argument may rest on properties that only hold
  locally. The rule it generates:

  ▎ Any invariant enforced by an in-process data structure must be expressible 
  ▎ as a single atomic operation against a shared store — and the in-process 
  ▎ version must be a substitutable implementation of that operation, not a 
  ▎ shortcut that sidesteps it.

  The nuance worth keeping: the in-memory Map is a correct implementation for a
  single-instance deployment, which Local World genuinely is. What's
  illegitimate is using single-threadedness as the justification. So the
  interface exposes claim(key, fingerprint) → claimed | exists with no
  get-then-set anywhere — if it can't be done in one conditional write, the
  interface is wrong. That absence is the design.

  On your question, the layered answer (§3):

  - L1 — atomic claim before start(). Gives I4 (exactly one run). Fails under
  store partition or the crash window.
  - L2 — deterministic provider keys derived from bookingId, never from 
  run/attempt/time. Gives I1/I2 (one charge, one hold) even if L1 fails 
  entirely. This is the floor and must never be traded away.
  - L3 — losing runs converge and abort (WDK's hook.getConflict() finally used
  for what it's good at).
  - L4 — compare-and-set on decision transitions. This is the one your "just
  rely on provider idempotency" option can't cover: run A refunds while run B
  consumes the hold. Both calls are individually legitimate and individually
  idempotent, so no provider key saves you. Only a conditional state transition
  does.

  Two things fell out of writing it that I hadn't seen before:

  The claim/start crash window (§3.1) — an owner that dies between claiming the
  key and recording the runId leaves a permanently stuck booking, which is worse
  than a duplicate. Fix is a state machine (claimed → running → terminal) where
  only the intermediate state is time-bounded, with atomic takeover. It's safe
  because of L2 — if the original owner was slow rather than dead, identical
  provider keys mean no double side effect. The layers compose: L2's determinism
  is what makes L1's recovery path affordable. That's the trade to state in the
  README — we risk a redundant run rather than a stuck booking, because L2
  makes redundant runs cheap and nothing makes a stuck booking cheap.

  Ordering is forced, not chosen (§4.4) — with no cross-store atomicity, every
  provider call must be: record intent (with the exact key) → call → record
  outcome, where unknown is a first-class outcome. Recording intent after the
  call loses the fact that a charge may exist, and I1 becomes unenforceable on
  recovery.

  Three tests carry this beyond what the assignment asks: T-conc-2 (two
  independently constructed app instances sharing one store — catches any
  invariant that leaked into module-level state, which a single-instance test
  structurally cannot), T-conc-3 (L1 disabled by injection, proving L2 holds
  I1/I2 independently), T-conc-4 (two runs racing into compensation). T-conc-2
  is cheap and is the one that makes the production claim testable rather than
  asserted.

  One thing I'd flag for PROCESS.md: Temporal would give you L1 natively via
  WorkflowId + reuse policy, in the runtime's own storage. That's a genuine
  capability difference from WDK, not a preference — worth naming, since it's
  the honest cost of the runtime choice.

  Want me to write §5–§7 next (happy path, failure taxonomy, test matrix), or
  review the larger plan first?

      506 +**Resolution — apply the same dev/production split as §1.1.** 
          +Durability is a                                               
      507 +*deployment* property selected by `WORKFLOW_TARGET_WORLD`, not
          + something our code                                           
      508 +implements or should try to compensate for. In development the
          + expectation is explicitly                                    
      509 +**not** persistence: in-memory queue, in-memory stores, epheme
          +ral state, fast tests. In                                     
      510 +production the identical workflow code runs on a World that do
          +es persist and resume.                                        
      511  
      512 +What this buys us: we write **zero** recovery code. No journal
          + replay, no boot-time                                         
      513 +resume, no restart tests. That work is the runtime's, and reim
          +plementing it locally would                                   
      514 +be precisely the hand-rolled durability we rejected in D1 — wi
          +th the added flaw of testing                                  
      515 +a mechanism that never runs in production.                    
      516 +                                                              
      517 +What we owe in exchange is precision about which layer owns wh
          +ich guarantee, stated in the                                  
      518 +README rather than left implicit:                             
      519 +                                                              
      520 +| Property | Owner | Local | Vercel/Postgres World |          
      521 +|---|---|---|---|                                             
      522 +| Step results persisted, no re-execution on replay | Runtime 
          +| ✅ | ✅ |                                                   
      523 +| At-least-once step execution | Runtime | ✅ | ✅ |          
      524 +| Interrupted runs resume after crash/restart | **World** | ❌
          + | ✅ |                                                       
      525 +| Idempotency claim survives restart | **Our store impl** | ❌
          + (in-memory) | ✅ (Redis/PG) |                                
      526 +| No double charge / double hold | **Our design (L2)** | ✅ | 
          +✅ |                                                          
      527 +                                                              
      528 +The last row is the one that matters: **L2 does not depend on 
          +any of the rows above it.**                                   
      529 +Deterministic provider keys hold under crash, restart, replay,
          + and duplicate runs alike,                                    
      530 +in every World. That is why it is the floor (`CORRECTNESS.md` 
          +§3.2), and why losing                                         
      531 +local persistence costs us nothing in correctness.            
      532 +                                                              
      533 +So: A8 is closed. We neither build nor claim restart recovery,
          + and the README states which                                  
      534 +World would provide it instead of implying we would.

      152

      154  
      155 +#### L2 hazard: execution identity is not booking identity    
      156 +                                                              
      157 +WDK documents `getStepMetadata().stepId` as the way to build i
          +dempotency keys for external                                  
      158 +APIs, and for the common case it is reasonable — `stepId` is s
          +table across retries *and*                                    
      159 +replays within a run.                                         
      160 +                                                              
      161 +**We must not use it.** `stepId` is stable within a run and un
          +ique between runs. Every                                      
      162 +scenario in which L2 is load-bearing is a scenario in which tw
          +o runs exist:                                                 
      163 +                                                              
      164 +| Scenario | `stepId`-derived key | `bookingId`-derived key | 
      165 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      166 +| Retry within one run | same ⇒ safe | same ⇒ safe |          
      167 +| Replay after crash | same ⇒ safe | same ⇒ safe |            
      168 +| L1 failed, two runs | **differs ⇒ double charge** | same ⇒ s
          +afe |                                                         
      169 +| §3.1 claim takeover | **differs ⇒ double charge** | same ⇒ s
          +afe |                                                         
      170 +| WDK native dedup (starts 2 runs by design) | **differs ⇒ dou
          +ble charge** | same ⇒ safe |                                  
      171 +                                                              
      172 +`stepId` protects against the runtime re-executing a step. It 
          +cannot protect against *us*                                   
      173 +running the booking twice, which is the actual risk. **The ide
          +mpotency key must be derived                                  
      174 +from the identity of the thing in the world, never from the id
          +entity of the execution                                       
      175 +attempting it.**                                              
      176 +                                                              
      177 +This generalises past this codebase: any idempotency key sourc
          +ed from execution context —                                   
      178 +run id, attempt number, task id, lambda request id — silently 
          +degrades to no protection at                                  
      179 +exactly the moment protection is needed.                      
      180 +                                                              
      181 +**Enforcement:** key derivation lives in one pure function tak
          +ing `bookingId` and a literal                                 
      182 +step name, with no access to workflow or step metadata. It is 
          +unit-tested for stability                                     
      183 +against a frozen expected-value table, so a future change that
          + reintroduces execution                                       
      184 +context fails a test rather than a customer.                  
      185 +

           dictory progress)

           ter a crashed claimant

● C2 was wrong, and correcting it removed work rather than adding it. The
  observability page documents only the CLI and web UI, but the API lives under
  the World SDK:

  const world = await getWorld();                       // workflow/runtime
  await world.events.list({ runId });                   // events are the source
  of truth
  await world.steps.list({ runId });                    // status, attempt, 
  input, output, error, retryAfter
  hydrateResourceIO(step, observabilityRevivers);       // 
  workflow/observability — devalue deserialization

  The consequence that matters: a step's persisted input/output already is the 
  provider-call record. With hold(offerId, idempotencyKey) → Hold, the framework
  has durably stored what was sent, what came back, and the key used — which is
  verbatim what the assignment asks the timeline to show. attempt and
  retryAfter give retry history free. So we build no bespoke provider-call log;
  this is precisely the "artifacts you're already producing" the brief was
  pointing at.

  The timeline becomes a merge of two sources, which is a real design point
  rather than a fudge: WDK's event log for execution facts, and our own records
  for everything that happens with no run at all — a 409 fingerprint conflict, a
  replayed response, a claim takeover. Those can't come from WDK because no
  workflow ever ran. I noted the two constraints the merge imposes (explicit
  source on each entry, since the clocks are independent; redaction at
  projection time, since step I/O is projected wholesale).

  A trap that fell out of the same reading, now C2.1 + a CORRECTNESS §3 
  subsection. WDK's docs recommend getStepMetadata().stepId as your external-API
  idempotency key. We must not use it. stepId is stable across retries and
  replays within a run, and different between runs — so it protects against the
  runtime re-executing a step, but not against us running the booking twice,
  which is the actual risk. Every scenario where L2 is load-bearing (L1 failure,
  §3.1 takeover, WDK's own native dedup which starts two runs by design) is a
  two-run scenario, and in all three stepId yields two keys and two charges. The
  general form is worth stating in PROCESS.md: any idempotency key sourced from
  execution context degrades to no protection at exactly the moment protection 
  is needed. Enforcement is a pure derivation function with no access to step
  metadata, unit-tested against a frozen value table.

  C3 — agreed, and applied. Durability is a deployment property selected by
  WORKFLOW_TARGET_WORLD, not something we implement. Dev is explicitly
  ephemeral; we write zero recovery code, because reimplementing it locally
  would be the hand-rolled durability we rejected in D1, with the extra flaw of
  testing a mechanism production never runs. Both docs now carry an ownership
  table, and the bottom row is the load-bearing one: I1/I2 depend on none of the
  rows above them, so losing local persistence costs liveness, never
  correctness.

  One more consequence I'd flag because it constrains a signature: since WDK
  persists step input at step start, §4.4's "record intent before the call" is
  discharged by the runtime — but only if the idempotency key is passed into the
  step as an argument rather than computed inside the body. A key computed
  inside isn't in the persisted input, so on recovery the intent record can't
  tell you which charge to go looking for. Same rule that rules out stepId,
  arrived at independently.

  Ready for §5–§7 of CORRECTNESS.md (happy path, failure taxonomy, test matrix),
  or do you want to review the larger plan first?

● Update(PLAN.md)
  ⎿  Added 91 lines
      383  fake the framework's internal clock, which we do not control. 
           Every attempt and its
      384  classification is recorded in our timeline (A12).
      385  
      386 +### A16 — The compensating calls take no idempotency key `OPEN
          +` (leaning: extend)                                           
      387 +                                                              
      388 +Caught by re-reading the assignment's own interfaces. The forw
          +ard calls take a key; the                                     
      389 +compensating and finalising calls do **not**:                 
      390 +                                                              
      391 +```ts                                                         
      392 +hold(offerId: string, idempotencyKey: string): Promise<Hold>; 
          +  // keyed ✅                                                 
      393 +charge(amountCents, currency, idempotencyKey): Promise<Charge>
          +; // keyed ✅                                                 
      394 +release(holdId: string): Promise<void>;                       
          +  // unkeyed ❗                                               
      395 +consume(holdId: string): Promise<void>;                       
          +  // unkeyed ❗                                               
      396 +refund(chargeId: string): Promise<void>;                      
          +  // unkeyed ❗                                               
      397 +```                                                           
      398 +                                                              
      399 +This directly threatens L2, which is the floor of the whole de
          +sign — and every unkeyed                                      
      400 +call is a *retryable step with a real-world side effect*, i.e.
          + precisely the case the                                       
      401 +assignment flags under "what to do when a step 'should be' ret
          +ryable but has a real-world                                   
      402 +side effect". Silently ignoring it would be the single largest
          + hole in the plan.                                            
      403 +                                                              
      404 +Two defensible answers:                                       
      405 +                                                              
      406 +- **(a) Extend the interface** to accept an idempotency key, a
          +s the assignment explicitly                                   
      407 +  invites ("Extend either interface if it makes the exercise r
          +icher"). Uniform with the                                     
      408 +  forward path, and L2 applies unchanged.                     
      409 +- **(b) Argue these are naturally idempotent by resource ident
          +ity.** `release(holdId)` and                                  
      410 +  `refund(chargeId)` name a specific resource and are state tr
          +ansitions to a terminal                                       
      411 +  state, so a correct provider treats a repeat as a no-op. Rea
          +l providers largely behave                                    
      412 +  this way — Stripe's refund endpoint is keyed, but releasing 
          +an already-released hold is                                   
      413 +  universally a no-op.                                        
      414 +                                                              
      415 +**Leaning (a), with (b) also made true.** Add the key paramete
          +r *and* implement the mocks                                   
      416 +so repeats are no-ops keyed on resource state. The reasoning: 
          +(b) alone rests on the                                        
      417 +provider being well-behaved, which is exactly the assumption t
          +his exercise punishes. But                                    
      418 +(b) is what protects us when a *real* provider ignores our key
          +, so we want both. The                                        
      419 +refund path is the one that must not fail — a double refund is
          + a real financial loss and,                                   
      420 +unlike a double charge, is not something the customer will rep
          +ort.                                                          
      421 +                                                              
      422 +Whichever way this lands, the **mock providers must model the 
          +unkeyed-repeat case                                           
      423 +explicitly**, so a test can prove our behaviour is safe rather
          + than assumed.                                                
      424 +                                                              
      425 +### A17 — `Charge.status: 'pending'` is in the given contract 
          +and we have no design for it `OPEN`                           
      426 +                                                              
      427 +The assignment's `Charge` type admits `'succeeded' | 'failed' 
          +| 'pending'`. Our design so                                   
      428 +far treats a charge as binary. A `pending` charge is a genuine
          +ly distinct state: the money                                  
      429 +is neither captured nor released, the provider will resolve it
          + asynchronously, and *we                                      
      430 +cannot know the outcome by retrying* — retrying returns `pendi
          +ng` again.                                                    
      431 +                                                              
      432 +This matters more than it first looks, because it is the one f
          +ailure mode where L2 does                                     
      433 +not help. A retry with the same key faithfully returns the sam
          +e `pending` record. There is                                  
      434 +no read-repair available; only waiting.                       
      435 +                                                              
      436 +Options:                                                      
      437 +                                                              
      438 +- **Treat `pending` as terminal-unknown.** Booking rests in an
          + explicit non-terminal state,                                 
      439 +  response says so (A1's `pending` arm carries it naturally), 
          +timeline shows why. Cheapest,                                 
      440 +  honest.                                                     
      441 +- **Wait for resolution via a hook/webhook** — this is exactly
          + what N2 is for, and it turns                                 
      442 +  the nice-to-have into the natural answer for a state the con
          +tract already forces on us.                                   
      443 +  `createWebhook` + provider callback resumes the run.        
      444 +- **Poll with `sleep()`** inside the workflow until it resolve
          +s or a budget expires. Durable                                
      445 +  and simple; less elegant than a webhook but far cheaper to b
          +uild.                                                         
      446 +                                                              
      447 +**Leaning:** treat `pending` as an explicit non-terminal state
          + now (cheap, satisfies I7),                                   
      448 +and let N2's webhook resume be the stretch that resolves it. T
          +his is the strongest                                          
      449 +argument yet for promoting N2, because it stops being a bolt-o
          +n demo and becomes the answer                                 
      450 +to a state the provider contract already contains. Decide alon
          +gside A4 and D3.                                              
      451 +                                                              
      452 +### A18 — Steps are isolated routes, so request-scoped state c
          +annot reach them `OPEN` — verify in spike                     
      453 +                                                              
      454 +WDK compiles steps into isolated API routes and dispatches the
          +m through a queue. It follows                                 
      455 +that a step body **cannot** see request headers, `AsyncLocalSt
          +orage` context, or anything                                   
      456 +else scoped to the HTTP request that started the run. Everythi
          +ng a step needs must arrive                                   
      457 +as workflow input or module-level state.                      
      458 +                                                              
      459 +Two places this bites, both currently under-specified:        
      460 +                                                              
      461 +1. **A9's `X-Chaos` header.** Per-request failure configuratio
          +n must be serialised into the                                 
      462 +   **workflow arguments**, not read ambiently inside the provi
          +der. Otherwise it silently                                    
      463 +   does nothing when a step runs out-of-band — a failure mode 
          +that would make the demo                                      
      464 +   quietly lie.                                               
      465 +2. **Mock provider state.** The providers hold in-memory charg
          +es and holds that both the                                    
      466 +   API layer and the steps must observe. On Local World (singl
          +e process, in-memory queue)                                   
      467 +   a module-level singleton works. That is an assumption about
          + the execution model, not a                                   
      468 +   guarantee, and it is exactly the kind of local-only crutch 
          +§1.1 warns about. In                                          
      469 +   production the provider would be a real HTTP service and th
          +e question dissolves.                                         
      470 +                                                              
      471 +**Resolution:** pass chaos configuration explicitly as workflo
          +w input; keep provider state                                  
      472 +in a module singleton with a comment naming it as a stand-in f
          +or a remote service; and                                      
      473 +**verify both in the D2 spike** before building on them. If st
          +eps turn out to run in a                                      
      474 +separate process even locally, the provider mock needs a share
          +d store and the plan changes                                  
      475 +shape — which is why this is spike-blocking rather than a buil
          +d-time detail.

      258 +### 3.3 Where L2 does not reach                               
      259 +                                                              
      260 +Two cases in the assignment's own provider contract fall outsi
          +de L2's protection. Both are                                  
      261 +`OPEN` in `PLAN.md`; recorded here because they are gaps in a 
          +*guarantee*, not merely                                       
      262 +undecided design.                                             
      263 +                                                              
      264 +**(a) The compensating calls carry no idempotency key.** `rele
          +ase(holdId)`,                                                 
      265 +`consume(holdId)`, and `refund(chargeId)` are unkeyed in the g
          +iven interface, while                                         
      266 +`hold()` and `charge()` are keyed. Every one of them is a retr
          +yable step with an                                            
      267 +irreversible real-world effect, so L2 as stated does not cover
          + the compensation path — the                                  
      268 +exact path I3 depends on. Until `PLAN.md` A16 is resolved, **I
          +3 is guaranteed only to the                                   
      269 +extent that repeat calls are no-ops by resource identity**, wh
          +ich is an assumption about                                    
      270 +provider behaviour rather than something we enforce. A double 
          +refund is the worst case: a                                   
      271 +real financial loss that, unlike a double charge, no customer 
          +will report.                                                  
      272 +                                                              
      273 +**(b) A `pending` charge cannot be resolved by retrying.** The
          + given `Charge` type admits                                   
      274 +`'pending'`. L2 turns a retry into a read-repair, but read-rep
          +airing a pending charge                                       
      275 +returns `pending` again — the information does not exist yet a
          +t the provider. This is the                                   
      276 +one failure mode where *no* idempotency scheme helps, because 
          +the ambiguity is in the                                       
      277 +world, not in our knowledge of it. The only correct responses 
          +are to wait (webhook or                                       
      278 +poll) or to represent the uncertainty honestly in the response
          + and the timeline (I7).                                       
      279 +                                                              
      280 +Both belong in the failure taxonomy (§6) when it is written, a
          +nd neither should be allowed                                  
      281 +to disappear into "the mocks always return succeeded".        
      282 +                                                              
      283  ## 4. Execution model assumptions
      284  
      285  ### 4.1 What we may assume

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Committed as dec12a2. Here's the assessment.

  What I fixed in this pass

  One outright contradiction. A8 still said "our booking store and idempotency
  records are written to disk, so timelines survive a restart" — written before
  the C3 dev/prod split, which says local state is in-memory and ephemeral. The
  C3 decision is the right one; A8 now defers to it.

  One stale conclusion. M8's constraint text still said the audit requirement
  "pushes us toward a runtime whose execution journal we own" — a leftover from
  when I was arguing for hand-rolled. C2 killed that reasoning.

  One test that would have been flaky and wrong. A11 step 3 proposed counting
  workflow entries via a counter incremented inside the first step. Steps retry
  — that counter over-counts on any transient failure, so a test claiming
  "exactly one run" would fail on a booking that ran once and retried. Replaced
  with world.runs.list(), the runtime's own registry, which is both correct and
  stronger evidence than anything we instrument ourselves.

  Three genuine gaps found against the assignment's own types

  A16 — the compensating calls are unkeyed. hold() and charge() take an
  idempotency key; release(holdId), consume(holdId), and refund(chargeId) do
  not. Every one is a retryable step with an irreversible effect, so L2 — the
  floor of the entire design — does not currently cover the compensation path,
  which is exactly the path I3 depends on. This is also verbatim the
  assignment's "what to do when a step should be retryable but has a real-world
  side effect." Leaning: extend the interface (the brief explicitly invites it)
  and make the mocks no-op on repeat, since relying on provider good behaviour
  is the assumption this exercise punishes.

  A17 — Charge.status includes 'pending', and we had no design for it. This is
  the one failure mode where L2 provides nothing: retrying with the same key
  faithfully returns pending again, because the information doesn't exist yet at
  the provider. It also promotes N2 from bolt-on demo to the natural answer for
  a state the contract already forces on us.

  A18 — steps are isolated routes, so request-scoped state can't reach them.
  A9's X-Chaos header must be serialised into workflow arguments; read ambiently
  inside a provider it would silently do nothing when the step runs
  out-of-band, making the demo quietly lie. Same question applies to mock
  provider state. Spike-blocking, not author-blocking.

  Honest assessment

  The strong parts: the layered L1–L4 idempotency model with an explicit "what
  still holds when this layer fails" table; the ownership split for durability;
  two reasoned departures from WDK's own documented guidance. That last one is
  unusually good material for the annotated-prompts section, which is the
  highest-weighted criterion.

  The real risk is scope, not correctness. This plan has grown past two hours of
  build. §8's decomposition, refund compensation, pending handling, a merged
  two-source timeline, and T-conc-2/3/4 is more than the budget. The brief says
  cutting intelligently is the exercise, so §13 needs to be a deliberate cut
  list written before building, not a post-hoc apology. My instinct: build
  L1/L2/L4 and the four quadrants properly, ship T-conc-1/2/3, and cut hold
  expiry, N3, and possibly N2 unless A17 pulls it in.

  The unquantified risk is D2's spike. Everything rests on WDK's bundler plugin
  composing with Vitest, and I found no testing documentation at all. If that's
  awkward, the test strategy — which is most of the grade — changes shape. It
  should be the first thing built.

  Still open: A1 (API shape), A4 (compensation strength), A12 (timeline format),
  A16, A17, D2. A1 and D2 block the most.

  One thing I'd flag as my own uncertainty: I have not run a single line of WDK.
  Everything about it here is from documentation, including the claim that
  world.runs.list() is queryable from a test process against the Local World.
  That specific assumption carries the concurrency test, so it's worth verifying
  first in the spike.

✻ Baked for 3m 24s

  the nice to haves to a nice to have section of the plan, so we can implement  
  core first. Anything else, lets bias towards doing just the core, but any     
  thing that really improves our example, shows how we would make things        
  correct, should stay.

✻ Cooked for 5m 21s

❯ Nice work. Reading through the PLAN.md it is looking good. There is a lot of  
  complicated consistancy logic. It is key to be correct here, and have a       
  properly architected solution. Are there any gaps in the plan? What do you    
  need before we start implementation?                                          

● Noted on the tool — I'll use Edit for those. There are 5 stale
  cross-references left from the nice-to-have renumbering (N2/N3 pointing at the
  old scheme, and §6's "to be written" list still numbering sections §7–§13
  that now collide with the real §7–§9). Cosmetic, but I'll clean them up in the
  next pass.

  On the substantive question — yes, there are gaps, and one is serious.

  The serious gap: there is no state machine

  The plan asserts L4 ("every transition authorising an irreversible action is a
  conditional write on the booking's current state") but never enumerates the 
  states or the legal transitions. I've been using pending, charged,
  compensating, compensation_failed, needs-reconciliation, held, consumed
  informally across three documents without ever defining the set. You can't CAS
  on a state machine you haven't written down, and L4 is the layer that
  actually protects I3.

  Closely related and equally missing: M6's four-quadrant matrix is never mapped
  to outcomes. It's a headline must-have, and nowhere in either document does a
  table say quadrant → what we do → terminal state → HTTP response. That's
  CORRECTNESS.md §6, still a stub. Everything else in the plan is scaffolding
  around this table; it should exist before any code does.

  Smaller gaps, roughly by cost of discovering them late

  Order of operations is assumed, never argued. §2.1 says hold → charge →
  consume without justifying it. Hold-first is right — inventory is the scarce
  resource, and releasing a hold is cheaper and safer than refunding money — but
  it should be reasoned, since reviewers grade decomposition. And "booked" is
  never defined; I believe it means the hold reached consumed, but that's
  inference from the given Hold type.

  Claim-before-validate or validate-before-claim? Unaddressed, and it's a
  genuine fork. Claim first and a malformed body permanently burns the
  idempotency key. Validate first and you've got a window where two concurrent
  requests both validate before either claims. Standard answer is validate →
  claim, but it means a rejected request's fingerprint is never stored, so the
  second attempt with the same key and a corrected body succeeds rather than
  409ing. That's probably correct, but it should be a decision.

  The loser's join path has an unspecified race. L1 says the losing request
  "joins the in-flight run and awaits the same outcome". Mechanically it reads
  the record to get runId — but there's a window where the winner has claimed
  and not yet written runId. The loser then has to wait for it. That's the exact
  code path T-conc-1 exercises, and it's currently one sentence of prose. It
  also rests on an unverified assumption: that getRun(runId).returnValue works
  from a different request context than the one that called start(). If it
  doesn't, the whole sync-response design for duplicates changes.

  One store or two? The idempotency record (key, fingerprint, bookingId, runId,
  state, stored response) and the booking record overlap heavily. Never stated
  whether they're one thing. Affects whether L1's claim and L4's CAS touch the
  same row.

  Key derivation covers three of five calls. L2 gives keys for hold, charge,
  refund — not consume or release, which A16 established are equally
  side-effecting.

  What I need before implementation

  First, a 15-minute spike — this is unchanged and still the top risk, but it
  now has a specific question list: does WDK's bundler plugin compose with
  Vitest; can a step see request-scoped state (A18); do module-singleton
  providers work across the step boundary; does getRun().returnValue work
  cross-request; is world.runs.list() queryable from a test process. Every one
  of those is currently an assumption from documentation, and three of them can
  invalidate design decisions rather than just cost time.

  Four decisions from you, in the order they block work:

  - A1 — sync / sync-with-deadline / async. Blocks the response contract and
  every test's shape.
  - A4 — auto-compensate, or explicit terminal state only. Blocks the state
  machine.
  - A16 — extend the provider interface with idempotency keys, or rely on
  resource-identity no-ops.
  - D2 — Hono vs Express (I'd take Hono, for app.request() in tests without
  binding a port).

  Then let me write CORRECTNESS.md §5/§6 — happy path, state machine, and the
  quadrant table — before we write code. A4 and A16 feed directly into it, so
  it's the natural next artifact once those land.

  One honest caution: the plan has grown well past two hours of build. The
  consistency logic is correct-by-construction on paper, but L1 + L2 + L4 +
  compensation + a merged timeline + the test set is a lot. I'd rather cut §2.2
  entirely and one of A4's arms than ship all of it half-built — but that's a
  call worth making deliberately once the spike tells us what WDK actually
  costs.

✻ Cooked for 55s

❯ Ok, is the lack of state machine because we have expanded scope? I like the   
  reasoning for not using stepId, I wonder if the single threaded/prod          
  discussion the company may be thinking we do something simple and internal,   
  if this is the case, as they call out dont use postgres, and I dont see any   
  call for more stateful services, we may want to do something "development"    
  and locally in nodejs, and document this as only a single server solution.    
  thoughts?                                                                     

● Two separate answers, and on the second one I think you're right.

  On the state machine — no, that's not scope creep

  It's a genuine omission, and it would be needed just as much in the simplest
  possible version. The state machine is the four-quadrant matrix; M6 has no
  meaning without it. If anything, the distributed framing helped hide the gap —
  I spent attention on the coordination layer and never wrote the thing the
  core requirement actually rests on.

  On single-server — agreed, and the evidence is stronger than I treated it

  The assignment says it almost explicitly:

  - "Promise.all with two requests is plenty — no distributed-systems setup 
  needed"
  - "Persistence beyond what fits in memory or a JSON file"
  - "Production concerns … out of scope"
  - "Don't over-build."

  I over-read your earlier push. You were right that "Node is single-threaded"
  is a bad justification — but I turned that into "therefore build for N
  instances," which is a different and unrequested thing. The correct conclusion
  was narrower: be explicit about the deployment assumption instead of relying 
  on it silently. A single-process solution that names its constraint is honest;
  one that quietly depends on it is not. That distinction survives; the
  multi-instance machinery doesn't.

  The important thing I got wrong, though, is what "each layer" means. The
  grading criterion says "How you handled idempotency at each layer of the 
  system", and the brief's own framing is "Where idempotency lives — at the API 
  layer, the workflow layer, the step layer, or some combination." That's our
  layers, not distributed ones. So the L1–L4 model isn't distributed-systems
  overreach — it's answering exactly the question asked:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │          Layer           │     Maps to      │      Still needed        │
  │                          │                  │     single-process?      │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ L1 — claim before        │ API layer        │ Yes — this is M7         │
  │ start()                  │                  │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ L3 — losing-run          │ Workflow layer   │ Already deferred         │
  │ convergence              │                  │                          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ L4 — guard on decision   │ Domain/state     │ Yes — it's the state     │
  │ transitions              │ layer            │ machine                  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ L2 — deterministic       │ Step/provider    │ Yes — retries need it    │
  │ provider keys            │ layer            │ regardless               │
  └──────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  None of that requires more than one process. It gets simpler, not smaller: L1
  becomes a Map claim plus an in-flight promise map; L4 becomes an ordinary
  guarded transition rather than a CAS ceremony. L2 is completely untouched — it
  was never a distributed concern, it's a provider concern, and it's the heart
  of the exercise.

  What I'd cut

  - §1.2 rule 2 — "ship two implementations of every store." Replaced by one
  line naming the primitive a distributed version would use.
  - T-conc-2 — the two-instances-sharing-a-store test. It proves a property
  we're no longer claiming.
  - The Redis/Postgres/DynamoDB tables — collapse to a sentence.
  - The ownership tables in C3 / §4.5 — shrink to a short "what this does and
  doesn't do" list.
  - §1.1's framing — from "production-shaped interfaces" to "single-process by
  design, stated plainly, with the one seam that would need to change."

  What I'd keep

  L1, L2, L4, the stepId reasoning (unaffected — it's about execution vs.
  booking identity, not distribution), and T-conc-3 (L1 disabled, prove L2 holds
  independently). That last one is cheap and it's the best evidence that the
  layering is real rather than decorative.

  Net effect: less code, less doc, and the README gains an honest
  "single-server, in-memory, here's what would change at scale" section — which
  reads as judgment rather than as a gap.

  Want me to apply that reframing across both docs, then write the state machine
  and the quadrant table? That's the sequence I'd pick — the simplification
  makes the state machine smaller, so doing it first saves rework.

✻ Churned for 59s

      123 +  turn as the claim**, so a loser is guaranteed to find either
          + the promise or a terminal                                    
      124 +  record — never a claimed record with nothing to await. Getti
          +ng this ordering wrong

      306  ### 4.1 What we may assume
      307  
      308 -- The idempotency store and booking store are **linearizable f
          -or single-key conditional                                     
      309 -  writes**. Redis, Postgres, and DynamoDB all provide this. It
          - is the weakest assumption                                    
      310 -  that makes L1 and L4 work, and we assume nothing stronger — 
          -in particular, **no                                           
      311 -  multi-key transactions and no cross-store atomicity**.      
      312 -- Providers are idempotent on the keys we supply, and their id
          -empotency is durable                                          
      313 -  (assignment M4).                                            
      308 +- **One process.** Stated, not silent (§1). Synchronous sectio
          +ns do not interleave.                                         
      309 +- Providers are idempotent on the keys we supply (assignment M
          +4).                                                           
      310  - The workflow runtime executes each step **at least once**, a
           nd persists step results so
      311    completed steps are not re-executed on replay.
      312  
      313  ### 4.2 What we must not assume
      314  
      319 -- ❌ One process, one instance, or process-local state survivi
          -ng anything.                                                  
      320 -- ❌ Steps run exactly once. They do not. Every step must be s
          -afe to run twice.                                             
      321 -- ❌ A provider call that threw did not take effect (A10 — thi
          -s is the whole exercise).                                     
      322 -- ❌ Wall-clock ordering between instances, or synchronised cl
          -ocks.                                                         
      323 -- ❌ That a workflow, once started, will finish. Instances die
          -.                                                             
      315 +Being single-process removes the coordination hazards; it remo
          +ves none of the ones this                                     
      316 +exercise is actually about. All of the following remain true a
          +nd are where the bugs live:                                   
      317 +                                                              
      318 +- ❌ Steps run exactly once. They do not. **Every step must be
          + safe to run twice** — this                                   
      319 +  is a property of the runtime's retry semantics, not of how m
          +any servers there are.                                        
      320 +- ❌ A provider call that threw did not take effect (A10 — the
          + whole exercise).                                             
      321 +- ❌ That a workflow, once started, will finish. Steps exhaust
          + retries and runs fail.                                       
      322  - ❌ That the store and the provider can be updated atomically
            together. They cannot; this
      325 -  is why every provider call is bracketed by a durable record 
          -of *intent* before and                                        
      326 -  *outcome* after (I6).                                       
      323 +  is why every provider call is bracketed by a record of *inte
          +nt* before and *outcome*                                      
      324 +  after (§4.4, I6).                                           
      325 +- ❌ That "we only run one workflow" implies "each side effect
          + happens once". It does not —                                 
      326 +  retries alone can duplicate a side effect within a single ru
          +n. **L2 is what prevents                                      
      327 +  that, and it is needed even on one server.**                
      328  
      329 +The last point is the one to keep in view while simplifying: L
          +1 was the distributed-ish                                     
      330 +layer, and it collapses to a `Map`. L2 was never about distrib
          +ution at all.

● Fixed. Here's the plan as it now stands.

  The two documents

  PLAN.md (795 lines) — what we build, what we cut, why. CORRECTNESS.md (444) —
  what must remain true and how it breaks. Where they disagree, CORRECTNESS
  wins.

  The spine — what's decided

  Runtime: WDK, Local World. Zero-config, no Docker, deploys to Vercel
  unchanged. Four consequences drove real design (D1.1): start() takes no
  idempotency key so the framework can't satisfy M7 alone; there is a full
  run-history API (world.events/steps/runs) so the timeline projects it; stepId
  must not be the provider key; durability is a World property we don't
  reimplement.

  Single process, in-memory, said out loud. The discipline is one line:
  depending on single-process execution is fine, depending on it silently is 
  not.

  Idempotency in four layers, mapping onto the brief's own "API layer / workflow
  layer / step layer" framing:

  - L1 — Map claim before start(). No await between check and insert; that's the
  whole correctness argument. Gives I4.
  - L2 — provider keys derived from bkg:${bookingId}:${step}, never execution
  identity. Gives I1/I2 independent of everything above it. This is the floor.
  - L3 — losing-run convergence. Deferred.
  - L4 — guarded state transitions. Just reframed: this is the state machine,
  and it's what makes M6 well-defined. Its concurrency role is now third on its
  own list.

  Timeline = WDK's event/step log (execution facts) + our records (things
  happening with no run: conflicts, replays). No bespoke provider-call log —
  step input/output already is one.

  18 ambiguities logged, 13 decided. The good catches: A16 (the given interface
  leaves release/consume/refund unkeyed — a hole straight through L2's floor),
  A17 (Charge.status includes 'pending', the one case where no idempotency
  scheme helps), A18 (steps are isolated routes, so X-Chaos must be workflow
  input or it silently does nothing).

  Cuts are explicit (§7), eleven of them, each with a reason and a README flag.
  The one I'd defend hardest: the claim-takeover mechanism — we found the hole,
  can describe the fix precisely, and declined to ship recovery code no test
  here could exercise.

  What's still open — 5 decisions, yours

  ┌─────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │     │                 Decision                  │        Blocks         │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ A1  │ sync / sync-with-deadline / async         │ Response contract,    │
  │     │                                           │ every test's shape    │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ A4  │ auto-compensate vs explicit terminal      │ The state machine     │
  │     │ state                                     │                       │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ A16 │ extend provider interface with keys, or   │ Provider mocks, I3's  │
  │     │ rely on resource-identity no-ops          │ guarantee             │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ A17 │ how pending resolves                      │ State machine,        │
  │     │                                           │ whether N1 is in      │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ D2  │ Hono vs Express                           │ Everything            │
  └─────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  What's not written — and it's the important part

  CORRECTNESS.md §5/§6/§7 are stubs: happy path, failure taxonomy, test matrix.
  And PLAN.md §10 — the state machine — doesn't exist. Right now L4 guards
  transitions we haven't enumerated, and M6's four quadrants have no mapping to
  terminal states or HTTP responses.

  Everything else in 1,239 lines is scaffolding around a table that isn't there
  yet. A4 and A16 feed straight into it, which is why those two block most.

  Smaller unwritten items, all recorded in §6 so they're not discovered
  mid-code: order of operations is assumed but never argued; "booked" is
  undefined (presumably hold reaching consumed); validate-before-claim or
  claim-before-validate; one store or two; key derivation covers 3 of 5 provider
  calls.

  Honest risk

  Scope. This is still more than two hours of build even after the cutting pass.
  The core list in §2.1 is seven items and item 4 (compensation) is where it
  bloats. If it overruns, the thing to cut is A4's strong reading — ship the
  explicit terminal state, skip the auto-refund.

  The spike. Everything rests on assumptions from documentation. I have run zero
  lines of WDK. Five things need verifying before they can invalidate design
  rather than just cost time: Vitest + bundler plugin; can a step see request
  context (A18); do module-singleton providers work across the step boundary;
  does getRun().returnValue work cross-request (the loser's join path depends on
  it); is world.runs.list() queryable from a test process (T-conc-1 depends on
  it).

  Two of those — the last two — carry tests that are themselves graded
  requirements.

  Want me to take A1/A4/A16/A17/D2 now so I can write the state machine and the
  failure taxonomy? Or spike WDK first so we're deciding against verified
  ground? I'd lean spike-first, since A18 and the join path could change the
  answers. Also: shall I commit the simplification pass, or were you holding
  that deliberately?
