● Cut. Here's what changed and what's left.

  The four real cuts

  1. Automatic refund → deferred (N1). The assignment lists "Refund flow when 
  payment succeeded but inventory failed permanently" verbatim under "Nice to
  have (only if you have time)". I promoted it to must-have on the reasoning
  that the quadrant felt like the heart of the exercise. The brief had already
  told us where it sits. Core is now an explicit terminal charged_not_booked
  state naming the chargeId — that satisfies "never silently", which is what M6
  actually forbids.

  This is the big one: the state machine loses compensating and
  compensation_failed, the workflow loses its saga structure and rollback steps,
  and the test matrix loses a branch. Roughly a third of the remaining build.

  One distinction I'd flag as deliberate, not an inconsistency: releasing a hold
  when payment fails stays core. No money moves, it's one call, and without it
  the not-charged/not-booked quadrant leaves a hold dangling for no reason. Only
  the money remedy is deferred.

  2. CAS on state transitions → cut, state machine kept. L4's conditional writes
  guarded two runs racing a transition. With L1 working, L3 deferred, takeover
  cut, and the workflow body replayed deterministically from the runtime's event
  log, there's no path left to that race. Kept the enumerated states and
  transition table — which is what makes M6 testable — and dropped the ceremony.

  3. Our own event log for the timeline → cut. I had us emitting service-side
  events (replay served, 409, takeover) and merging two ordered streams with a
  source discriminator to handle independent clocks. That's a second audit log
  wearing a disguise — exactly what M8 says not to build. A 409 never creates a
  booking, so it has no timeline to appear in. Timeline is now WDK's step/event
  log plus the booking record's fields. The merge and the clock-ordering problem
  both disappear.

  4. Second concurrency test → deferred (N5). "L1 disabled, prove L2 holds
  independently" is the best evidence the layering is real, but it proves a
  design property rather than a requirement. T-conc-1 is the only required one.

  Plus two small ones: idempotency-key format validation (one trusted caller is
  assumed; the key only becomes a Map key), and the "two layers" framing for
  chaos config — it's one mechanism with a second entry point.

  Core is now six items

  1. POST /bookings — L1 claim, fingerprint replay/conflict
  2. Two mock providers, scripted failure modes
  3. Workflow: hold → charge → consume, deterministic L2 keys
  4. State machine — every M6 quadrant a named terminal state with a typed
  response arm
  5. GET /bookings/:id/timeline
  6. T-conc-1 + failure-matrix tests, README, PROCESS.md

  What I did not cut, and why

  The fingerprint/409 (A3) — ~10 lines and it serves grading criterion #3
  directly. Extending the provider interface with keys on release/consume/refund
  (A16) — three parameters, and it closes a hole straight through L2's floor.
  The research write-up in D1.1 — costs zero build time and it's most of
  PROCESS.md.

  Two decisions left that matter

  A1 — sync / sync-with-deadline / async. D2 — Hono vs Express. Everything else
  has a cheap default I'd just take: extend the provider interface (A16), treat
  pending as a named terminal state (A17), envelope format for the timeline
  (A12).

  Then the state machine, which is now small enough to write in one pass, and
  the spike.

✻ Sautéed for 4m 3s

❯ lets discuss A1 , this is POST /booking, whether to wait or return a 202?     
  Waiting alone isnt enough, we need to be able to handle disconnects,          
  rechecking, so we need async anyway. My question is whether to validate       
  inputs, record, and immedietly return a http 202, or, if not complicated,     
  happy path waits for up to 6-20 seconds, before returning a 202, but if we    
  have an answer before then it returns it immedietely. almost long polling. I  
  also want to just stay simple. Can you describe A4 the state machine choices? 

● A1 — your reasoning is right, and it simplifies things

  The async path has to exist regardless: a client can disconnect, and the
  timeline endpoint is already the re-check mechanism. So the wait isn't
  architecture, it's an optimization layered on top. That reframing settles it —
  long-poll costs almost nothing because the thing underneath it exists either
  way.

  Two things make me land on long-poll with a short deadline:

  It makes the tests simpler, not harder. With pure 202, every failure-matrix
  test becomes POST → poll until terminal → assert. That's a helper plus a loop
  in the bulk of the suite. With long-poll, it's POST → assert response arm.
  Since the providers are scripted and in-memory, the workflow completes in
  milliseconds — the deadline essentially never fires in tests, so the outcome
  is just there in the response.

  It's where grading criterion #6 lives. "The shape of the request and response,
  and whether the typed contract carries the right information out — including 
  in the failure cases." If POST always returns 202, every interesting failure
  arm moves to the timeline endpoint and the POST contract becomes trivial.
  Long-poll keeps the typed union on the endpoint being graded.

  And I need to correct something I wrote. CORRECTNESS.md §3 currently specifies
  an in-flight promise map so a duplicate request can join the winner's run,
  and calls it "the most subtle code in the service." That was over-engineering.
  The simple version:

  - Record is terminal → replay the stored response (needed for I5 regardless, 
  so it's not extra)
  - Record has a runId → Promise.race([getRun(runId).returnValue, deadline])
  - Record has no runId yet → return 202

  That last case is the race window I built the promise map for, and returning
  202 there is simply the honest answer — we genuinely don't know yet. No
  promise map, no ordering subtlety. I'll strip it.

  On duration: I'd go 5s, not 6–20s. Everything completes in milliseconds; the
  deadline only fires when something is genuinely stuck, and a reviewer curling
  doesn't want to wait 20 seconds to find that out. Nothing is lost — the
  timeline has the answer.

  The state machine

  Ordering first, because it eliminates a quadrant. With hold → charge → 
  consume, we only consume after a successful charge, so booked-without-charged 
  is unreachable by construction. That's worth stating explicitly in the README:
  one of M6's four quadrants is closed by the ordering rather than by handling.
  Charge-first would have made money the thing at risk instead of inventory —
  strictly worse.

  The genuine choice is granularity. Three options:

  (a) Outcome-only — pending, confirmed, failed, charged_not_booked. Four
  states, minimal. But the timeline can't answer "how far did it get before it
  died?" from the state field, and failed collapses "no inventory" and "card
  declined" into one bucket.

  (b) Progress-only — received → held → charged → consumed. Mirrors the steps,
  great for the timeline, but terminal outcomes are implied rather than named,
  so the response contract has to derive them. That's the derivation bug waiting
  to happen.

  (c) Progress + named terminals (recommended) — non-terminal states track how
  far we got; terminal states name the outcome:

  ┌───────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           │         State         │               Meaning               │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ in-flight │ pending               │ claimed, run started                │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ held                  │ inventory held, not yet charged     │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ charged               │ charged, not yet consumed           │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ terminal  │ confirmed             │ charged + consumed                  │
  │ ✅        │                       │                                     │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ terminal  │ inventory_unavailable │ hold failed — nothing charged,      │
  │ ❌        │                       │ nothing held                        │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ terminal  │ payment_failed        │ charge failed permanently; hold     │
  │ ❌        │                       │ released                            │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ terminal  │ charged_not_booked    │ the quadrant — charge succeeded,    │
  │ ⚠️        │                       │ consume failed permanently          │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ terminal  │ payment_pending       │ charge returned 'pending' (A17)     │
  │ ⚠️        │                       │                                     │
  └───────────┴───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  pending ──hold ok──> held ──charge ok──> charged ──consume ok──> confirmed
     │                  │                     │
     │ hold fails       │ charge fails        │ consume fails
     ▼                  ▼ (release hold)      ▼
  inventory_        payment_failed      charged_not_booked
  unavailable                                 │
                                              └─ charge returned pending ─>
  payment_pending

  Eight states, each with a response arm. Two sub-decisions inside it:

  Does a failed hold-release get its own state? If payment fails and release()
  also fails, there's a dangling hold. I'd say no — the hold expires on its own,
  no money is involved, and the timeline shows the failed release attempt.
  Adding a state for it buys nothing.

  Is payment_pending terminal? Under A17's cheap answer, yes — terminal-for-now,
  honestly reported, not resolved. If N2 (webhook) ever gets built it becomes
  non-terminal.

  Want me to write this into PLAN.md §10 with the transition table and the
  response arm for each state, and strip the promise map from CORRECTNESS.md §3?
  That plus the failure taxonomy would close the last real gap before the
  spike.

      165 -### A1 — Is `POST /bookings` synchronous or asynchronous? `OPE
          -N`                                                            
      165 +### A1 — Is `POST /bookings` synchronous or asynchronous? `DEC
          +IDED — (c), long-poll with a 5s deadline`                     
      166  
      167 +**Resolution: the service is asynchronous underneath; the POST
          + long-polls the result as an                                  
      168 +optimisation over that.**                                     
      169 +                                                              
      170 +The deciding argument is that the async path must exist regard
          +less — a client can                                           
      171 +disconnect mid-request, so it must be able to re-check, and `G
          +ET /bookings/:id/timeline`                                    
      172 +already is that mechanism. Given the async design exists eithe
          +r way, the wait is additive                                   
      173 +rather than architectural, and it costs one `Promise.race`.   
      174 +                                                              
      175 +Two reasons to spend that:                                    
      176 +                                                              
      177 +- **It makes the test suite smaller.** With pure 202, every fa
          +ilure-matrix test becomes                                     
      178 +  `POST → poll until terminal → assert`, i.e. a helper plus a 
          +loop through the bulk of the                                  
      179 +  suite. Long-polling puts the outcome in the response, so eac
          +h test is `POST → assert`.                                    
      180 +  Providers are scripted and in-memory, so runs complete in mi
          +lliseconds and the deadline                                   
      181 +  essentially never fires except when we make it.             
      182 +- **It is where grading criterion #6 lives** — *"whether the t
          +yped contract carries the                                     
      183 +  right information out, including in the failure cases"*. If 
          +POST always returns `202`,                                    
      184 +  every interesting failure arm migrates to the timeline endpo
          +int and the POST contract                                     
      185 +  becomes trivial.                                            
      186 +                                                              
      187 +**Deadline: 5 seconds, and it must be injectable.** Tests set 
          +it to ~50ms so the `202` arm                                  
      188 +can be exercised in a fast test (see A9's `slow` outcome and T
          +-deadline-1). 5s rather than                                  
      189 +the 20s first considered: nothing legitimately takes that long
          + here, the timeline holds the                                 
      190 +answer anyway, and a reviewer with curl should not wait 20 sec
          +onds to learn we are stuck.                                   
      191 +                                                              
      192 +Consequences recorded elsewhere: the duplicate-request join pa
          +th collapses to three cases                                   
      193 +with no promise map (`CORRECTNESS.md` §3/L1), and `202` become
          +s a first-class arm of the                                    
      194 +response union rather than a timeout.                         
      195 +                                                              
      196 +The original analysis follows, retained because the trade-off 
          +is worth showing in                                           
      197 +`PROCESS.md`.                                                 
      198 +                                                              
      199 +---

      861     reimplementing it locally.
      862  7. Catching that the given provider interface leaves compensat

      863  
      864 +## 10. Booking state machine `DECIDED`                        
      865 +                                                              
      866 +The definition of M6. Every quadrant of (charged | not) × (boo
          +ked | not) is a **named                                       
      867 +reachable state with its own response arm**, so "handles the m
          +atrix" is testable rather than                                
      868 +asserted. Referenced by `CORRECTNESS.md` §3/L4.               
      869 +                                                              
      870 +### 10.1 Ordering, and the quadrant it eliminates             
      871 +                                                              
      872 +**hold → charge → consume.** Inventory is the scarce resource 
          +and releasing a hold is                                       
      873 +cheaper and safer than moving money, so the reversible commitm
          +ent goes first.                                               
      874 +                                                              
      875 +The consequence is worth stating in the README: **we only cons
          +ume after a successful                                        
      876 +charge, so "booked but not charged" is unreachable by construc
          +tion.** One of M6's four                                      
      877 +quadrants is closed by the ordering rather than by handling it
          +. Charging first would have                                   
      878 +put money at risk instead of inventory — strictly worse for th
          +e same effort.                                                
      879 +                                                              
      880 +"Booked" means the hold reached `consumed`. A hold alone is a 
          +reservation, not a booking.                                   
      881 +                                                              
      882 +### 10.2 States                                               
      883 +                                                              
      884 +| | State | Meaning | HTTP |                                  
      885 +|---|---|---|---|                                             
      886 +| in-flight | `pending` | Claimed, run started, no provider ca
          +ll settled yet | `202` |                                      
      887 +| | `held` | Inventory held, not yet charged | `202` |        
      888 +| | `charged` | Charged, not yet consumed | `202` |           
      889 +| terminal ✅ | `confirmed` | Charged and consumed. The happy 
          +path | `200` |                                                
      890 +| terminal ❌ | `inventory_unavailable` | Hold failed. Nothing
          + held, nothing charged | `409` |                              
      891 +| terminal ❌ | `payment_failed` | Charge failed permanently; 
          +hold released | `402` |                                       
      892 +| terminal ⚠️ | `charged_not_booked` | **The quadrant.** Charg
          +e succeeded, consume failed permanently | `200` + explicit sta
          +te |                                                          
      893 +| terminal ⚠️ | `payment_pending` | Charge returned `'pending'
          +` (A17); outcome not yet knowable | `200` + explicit state |  
      894 +                                                              
      895 +The in-flight states return `202` only because the A1 deadline
          + expired — they are what a                                    
      896 +long-poll gives up on, not a separate contract.               
      897 +                                                              
      898 +`charged_not_booked` and `payment_pending` return `200`, not `
          +5xx`. They are outcomes we                                    
      899 +understand and report precisely, per A5 and I7. A `5xx` would 
          +say "something went wrong we                                  
      900 +cannot describe", which is false and would lose the informatio
          +n the caller needs.                                           
      901 +                                                              
      902 +### 10.3 Transitions                                          
      903 +                                                              
      904 +```                                                           
      905 +pending ──hold ok──> held ──charge ok──> charged ──consume ok─
          +─> confirmed                                                  
      906 +   │                  │                     │                 
      907 +   │ hold fails       │ charge fails        │ consume fails   
      908 +   │ (permanent)      │ (permanent)         │ (permanent)     
      909 +   ▼                  ▼  [release hold]     ▼                 
      910 +inventory_          payment_failed      charged_not_booked    
      911 +unavailable                                                   
      912 +                    charge -> 'pending'                       
      913 +                            ▼                                 
      914 +                     payment_pending                          
      915 +```                                                           
      916 +                                                              
      917 +Legal transitions, and nothing else:                          
      918 +                                                              
      919 +| From | To | On |                                            
      920 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      921 +| `pending` | `held` | `hold()` succeeded |                   
      922 +| `pending` | `inventory_unavailable` | `hold()` failed perman
          +ently |                                                       
      923 +| `held` | `charged` | `charge()` returned `succeeded` |      
      924 +| `held` | `payment_pending` | `charge()` returned `pending` |
      925 +| `held` | `payment_failed` | `charge()` failed permanently — 
          +**release the hold first** |                                  
      926 +| `charged` | `confirmed` | `consume()` succeeded |           
      927 +| `charged` | `charged_not_booked` | `consume()` failed perman
          +ently |                                                       
      928 +                                                              
      929 +Terminal states have no outgoing transitions. An attempted ill
          +egal transition throws — a                                    
      930 +bug, not a runtime condition.                                 
      931 +                                                              
      932 +### 10.4 Two sub-decisions, and why they went the way they did
      933 +                                                              
      934 +**A failed hold-release does not get its own state.** If `char
          +ge()` fails permanently and                                   
      935 +`release()` also fails, a hold dangles. It still resolves to `
          +payment_failed`: no money is                                  
      936 +involved, holds carry `expiresAt` and expire on their own, and
          + the timeline shows the failed                                
      937 +release attempt. A state for it would add a branch that buys n
          +othing.                                                       
      938 +                                                              
      939 +**`payment_pending` is terminal — for now.** Under A17's cheap
          + answer we report the                                         
      940 +uncertainty honestly rather than resolving it. If N2 (webhook 
          +resume) is ever built it                                      
      941 +becomes non-terminal, with `payment_pending → charged | paymen
          +t_failed`. That is the only                                   
      942 +transition the design would need to grow.                     
      943 +                                                              
      944 +### 10.5 Relationship to the idempotency record               
      945 +                                                              
      946 +The idempotency record's own lifecycle (`claimed → running → t
          +erminal`, `CORRECTNESS.md`                                    
      947 +§3/L1) is **separate and deliberately so**. It tracks *our exe
          +cution*; the booking state                                    
      948 +tracks *the world*. They fail independently — the asymmetry §2
          + of `CORRECTNESS.md` exists to                                
      949 +protect. A booking can be `confirmed` while the idempotency re
          +cord is still being written;                                  
      950 +the record stores the terminal response once known, for replay
          + under I5.                                                    
      951 +                                                              
      952  ## 9. Notes
      953  
      954  - `ASSIGNMENT.md` is gitignored and will not be published. `PL
           AN.md`, `CORRECTNESS.md`,

● Written up. Summary of what landed:

  A1 — decided: long-poll, 5s, injectable

  Your framing settled it: the async path exists regardless, so the wait is
  additive, not architectural. Two reasons to spend the one Promise.race: it
  makes the test suite smaller (POST → assert instead of POST → poll → assert
  through the whole failure matrix), and it keeps the typed union on the
  endpoint grading criterion #6 actually looks at.

  5s rather than 6–20s — nothing legitimately takes that long here, the timeline
  holds the answer, and a reviewer with curl shouldn't wait 20 seconds to learn
  we're stuck. The deadline is injectable so tests set it to ~50ms.

  I also stripped the promise map from CORRECTNESS.md §3. The duplicate-request
  join is now three cases: terminal → replay; has runId → race it; no runId yet
  → return 202. That third case is the race window I'd built machinery for, and
  202 is simply the honest answer. What I'd called "the most subtle code in the
  service" is gone.

  The 202 test — T-deadline-1

  Added, with slow(ms) as an explicit provider script outcome (the only one that
  sleeps). Four assertions, and the second is the one that matters:

  1. POST returns 202 + bookingId
  2. the workflow keeps running after the response — 202 is not abandonment
  3. the booking reaches a terminal state
  4. the timeline shows the whole run, so a caller who gave up on the long-poll
  can still find out what happened

  A 202 that silently dropped the work would pass a naive version of this test.
  That's exactly the failure the async design exists to avoid.

  The state machine — PLAN.md §10

  Eight states, option (c). The thing I'd put in the README: hold → charge → 
  consume means "booked but not charged" is unreachable by construction — one of
  M6's four quadrants is closed by ordering rather than handling. Charging
  first would have put money at risk instead of inventory for the same effort.

  charged_not_booked and payment_pending return 200 with an explicit state, not
  5xx. They're outcomes we understand; a 5xx would say "something went wrong we
  can't describe", which is false and discards what the caller needs.

  Also recorded: a failed hold-release gets no state of its own (holds expire,
  no money involved, timeline shows the attempt), and §10.5 keeps the booking
  state machine separate from the idempotency record's lifecycle — one tracks
  the world, the other tracks our execution, and they fail independently.

  D2 — decided: Hono + Vitest

  Worth deciding rather than deferring, and the reason is concrete:
  app.request() drives the app in-process without binding a port. Every test
  here is an HTTP test, and T-conc-1 fires two simultaneous requests — doing
  that against a real socket adds server lifecycle, port allocation, and
  flakiness unrelated to what we're testing. Express needs supertest or a live
  listener for the same coverage. Reversible in ~20 lines.

  What's left

  Two decisions, both with cheap answers I'd just take: A16 (add idempotency
  keys to release/consume/refund — three parameters, closes a hole through L2's
  floor) and A12 (envelope timeline format). Say the word and I'll take both.

  Then: the spike (five questions, two can change the decomposition), §11
  workflow decomposition, §12 API contracts, and CORRECTNESS.md §5–§7 — which
  §10 has made mechanical, since the terminal states now exist to map onto.

  Still uncommitted, by the way — the simplification and this pass are both
  sitting in the working tree.

✻ Churned for 2m 56s

❯ lets go over the two decisions, spike is writing code?                        

● Yes, the spike is code — the first we'd write. ~15 minutes, throwaway (or it
  becomes the skeleton): a Hono app, one workflow with two steps, one Vitest
  test. It answers five questions, and two of them can change the design rather
  than just cost time — whether a step can see request-scoped state (A18), and
  whether world.runs.list() is queryable from a test process (T-conc-1 depends
  on it entirely). It counts toward the budget.

  Now the two decisions.

  A16 — and I think I got this one wrong

  I flagged the unkeyed compensating calls as "the single largest hole in the
  plan." Looking harder, the given interface isn't sloppy — it's principled, and
  the pattern is consistent:

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Call          │ Keyed? │                  Why                   │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hold(offerId, key)    │ ✅     │ Creates a resource. No natural id      │
  │                       │        │ exists yet                             │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ charge(amount,        │        │ Creates a resource. You could          │
  │ currency, key)        │ ✅     │ legitimately charge the same amount    │
  │                       │        │ twice                                  │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ release(holdId)       │ ❌     │ Transitions a named resource: held →   │
  │                       │        │ released                               │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ consume(holdId)       │ ❌     │ Transitions a named resource: held →   │
  │                       │        │ consumed                               │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ refund(chargeId)      │ ❌     │ Transitions a named resource: charged  │
  │                       │        │ → refunded                             │
  └───────────────────────┴────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Creates need a key because there's nothing else to distinguish "retry" from
  "second call". Transitions don't, because the resource id already does —
  consume(hold_abc) twice isn't two consumes, it's one consume attempted twice,
  and the second finds it already consumed.

  The case I was worried about actually works fine: applied_then_lost on consume
  — the hold commits to consumed, then the call throws. The retry calls
  consume(hold_abc), finds it already consumed, returns success. No key
  required. Same for the double-refund I called "a real financial loss no
  customer will report" — the second refund finds the charge already refunded.

  So: option (b), rely on resource identity — but the mocks must enforce it.
  Each provider resource gets a small state machine, and a repeat call on an
  already-transitioned resource is a no-op returning success, not an error.
  release() on an already-consumed hold is a genuine error, though, and the mock
  should say so.

  That's less code than extending the interface, and it's better signal: it
  shows we worked out why the contract is shaped that way rather than
  "improving" it. My earlier framing — "extend it, the assignment invites
  extension" — was reaching for the more elaborate answer.

  The L2 key derivation table then covers exactly the two creates, which is the
  whole story:
  bkg:${bookingId}:hold      → inventory.hold
  bkg:${bookingId}:charge    → payment.charge

  A12 — envelope, and it's not close

  The assignment: "whether someone could actually diagnose a real issue from the
  output." NDJSON's advantages are streaming and volume; a booking has roughly
  ten events. The envelope answers "what state is this in, and why" in one
  response and subsumes the status endpoint we cut in A14.

  The sub-decision worth making explicit is what sits at the top:

  {
    "booking": { "id", "idempotencyKey", "fingerprint", "createdAt" },
    "outcome": { "state": "charged_not_booked", "reason": "consume failed after 
  4 attempts",
                 "chargeId": "ch_...", "holdId": "hold_...", "since": "..." },
    "runId": "run_...",
    "events": [ ... ]
  }

  That outcome block is the 2am answer before you read a single event — state,
  cause, and the identifiers you'd need to act. The events are there to confirm
  it and show the path. runId lets a reviewer cross-check with npx workflow 
  inspect run.

  Both are cheap. Want me to write them in — A16 as resource-identity with
  enforcing mocks, A12 as the envelope above — and then run the spike?

● Committed as 4e09ae7. (I see you'd committed f45e03d in between — that's
  preserved.)

  Both decisions written in

  A16 — leave the interface alone. The reusable idea, now in the README notes:
  idempotency keys are for creates; for transitions, the resource identity is 
  the key. hold and charge create — nothing else distinguishes a retry from a
  second call. release/consume/refund transition a named resource, which already
  does that work.

  The obligation this shifts onto us is small and testable: each provider
  resource gets a state machine, so a repeat call on an already-transitioned
  resource is a no-op returning success, and an illegal transition (release() on
  a consumed hold) is a genuine error. That converts "providers are probably
  well-behaved" into something the suite checks. Less code than extending the
  interface.

  A12 — minimal envelope. { booking, runId, events }, returning the booking
  record as it already is. Dropped the derived outcome block: every field was
  already on the record, so grouping them built a second representation to keep
  in sync for zero information gain.

  Planning is done

  Every author decision is closed — A1, A4, A8, A12, A16, A17, D1, D2. Two OPEN
  markers remain and neither is yours:

  - A13 — step granularity, resolved by writing §11 (the rule is already stated:
  a step boundary exists wherever an at-least-once retry would duplicate a
  real-world side effect).
  - A18 — whether a step can see request-scoped state. Answered by running code.

  The spike, concretely

  ~15 minutes, and it's the first code. A Hono app, one workflow with two steps,
  one Vitest test. Five questions:

  1. Does WDK's bundler plugin compose with Vitest at all? (if not, the whole 
  test strategy changes)
  2. Can a step see request-scoped state? (A18 — decides how chaos config 
  reaches the provider)
  3. Do module-singleton providers work across the step boundary? (decides 
  whether the mocks need a shared store)
  4. Does getRun(runId).returnValue work from a different request? (the 
  duplicate-join path in L1 depends on it)
  5. Is world.runs.list() queryable from a test process? (T-conc-1 depends on it
  entirely)

  4 and 5 each carry a graded requirement. If 5 fails we need a different way to
  assert the run count, and M7 says explicitly to verify it.

  Everything after the spike is mechanical: §11 decomposition, §12 contracts,
  CORRECTNESS.md §5–§7 (the state machine gave those their terminal states),
  then build in the §2.1 order.

  Want me to run the spike?

  I checked each spike question for whether it changes a decision or just gets

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            Question             │              If it fails              │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Vitest + bundler plugin         │ Tooling problem, not a design one —   │
  │ composes?                       │ found on the first test               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Can a step see request state    │ Already decided to pass chaos config  │
  │ (A18)?                          │ as a workflow argument, which is the  │
  │                                 │ safe answer either way                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                 │ Only bites if steps run               │
  │ Module-singleton providers      │ out-of-process; Local World is        │
  │ across the step boundary?       │ documented single-instance, in-memory │
  │                                 │  queue                                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ getRun(runId).returnValue       │ Degrades to the 202 we already return │
  │ cross-request?                  │  in the no-runId window               │
  │                                 │ (CORRECTNESS.md §3/L1)                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ world.runs.list() from a test?  │ Falls back to the counter at our      │
  │                                 │ start() call site (A11 item 3)        │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  So skipping it is fine. Nothing is blocked.

  One piece of sequencing that replaces it for free

  §2.1's core list is written in dependency order, which reads as horizontal
  layers — build the API, then providers, then the workflow. I'd invert the
  first pass: build the thinnest vertical slice first — one happy-path booking
  going POST → hold → charge → consume → 200, with a single test.

  That slice touches every one of the five unknowns as a side effect, at the
  point where they're cheapest to fix, and it leaves the service runnable from
  commit one (which CLAUDE.md asks for). Then fill in breadth: failure modes,
  the state machine's other seven states, concurrency, timeline.

  It's the same work in a different order, and it front-loads exactly the risk
  the spike was for.

  Where things stand

  Planning is complete and pushed. PLAN.md (~1000 lines) and CORRECTNESS.md
  (~490) carry every decision with its reasoning, 11 explicit cuts, and the
  state machine.

  Still to write, all mechanical now: §11 decomposition, §12 contracts, §13
  timeline schema, and CORRECTNESS.md §5–§7 — the last of which the state
  machine made straightforward, since the terminal states now exist to map the
  failure taxonomy onto.

  Say the word and I'll start on the vertical slice, or write out §11/§12 first
  if you'd rather have the decomposition on paper before any code.

● Good questions — two of them make me reverse an earlier call. Let me take them
  in order, then write the remaining sections.

  Q5/Q6: charged_not_booked should not be 200. I wrote "200, because it's an
  outcome we understand." That reasoning was wrong. The typed body is for the
  caller; the status code is for everything else in the path — proxies, retry
  middleware, dashboards, alerting. A 200 tells all of them "this succeeded,"
  for a state where we took money and delivered nothing. I7 says no terminal
  state is reached silently, and a 200 there is precisely silent to the
  operational tooling that already exists.

  And your needs_manual_intervention instinct is right, but as a flag rather 
  than a state — collapsing distinct causes into one bucket would lose the
  diagnosis.

  Q7: hold release — a field, not a state. Let me apply all three.

      1077 +                                                             
      1078 +## 11. Workflow decomposition `DECIDED` (closes A13)         
      1079 +                                                             
      1080 +Grading criterion #2. The rule from A13: **a step boundary ex
           +ists wherever an at-least-once                               
      1081 +retry would otherwise duplicate a real-world side effect.**  
      1082 +                                                             
      1083 +### 11.1 What is and is not a step                           
      1084 +                                                             
      1085 +| | Where | Why |                                            
      1086 +|---|---|---|                                                
      1087 +| Validation, fingerprinting, L1 claim, `bookingId` allocatio
           +n | **Before the workflow**, in the request handler | They mu
           +st happen before `start()`, and their results become workflow
           + arguments. Also they can reject without creating a run |    
      1088 +| Choosing the next state, deciding whether to release | **Wo
           +rkflow body** | Pure decisions. Must stay deterministic — no 
           +clocks, no randomness, no I/O |                              
      1089 +| `hold`, `charge`, `consume`, `release` | **One step each** 
           +| Each is exactly one real-world side effect |               
      1090 +| Persisting booking state transitions | **Inside the step th
           +at caused them** | Keeps intent/outcome adjacent to the call 
           +that produced it (`CORRECTNESS.md` §4.4) |                   
      1091 +                                                             
      1092 +**One provider call per step, never two.** Two side effects i
           +n one step means a retry after                               
      1093 +a partial failure re-runs the first — the exact bug this exer
           +cise is built around.                                        
      1094 +                                                             
      1095 +### 11.2 The steps                                           
      1096 +                                                             
      1097 +Every step is annotated in code with the answer to *"what doe
           +s a retry of this do in the                                  
      1098 +world?"*, because that is the question being graded.         
      1099 +                                                             
      1100 +| Step | Signature | Retry does what, in the world | `maxRetr
           +ies` | Fatal when |                                          
      1101 +|---|---|---|---|---|                                        
      1102 +| `holdStep` | `(offerId, idemKey) → Hold` | Returns the **sa
           +me** hold — the key makes it a read-repair | 3 | Offer unknow
           +n / sold out |                                               
      1103 +| `chargeStep` | `(amountCents, currency, idemKey) → Charge` 
           +| Returns the **same** charge. This is the one that must neve
           +r duplicate | 3 | Card declined |                            
      1104 +| `consumeStep` | `(holdId) → void` | No-op; the hold is alre
           +ady `consumed` (A16 — resource identity) | 5 | Hold expired o
           +r released |                                                 
      1105 +| `releaseStep` | `(holdId) → void` | No-op; the hold is alre
           +ady `released` | 2 | Hold already `consumed` |               
      1106 +                                                             
      1107 +Notes on the numbers, which are judgement rather than convent
           +ion:                                                         
      1108 +                                                             
      1109 +- `consumeStep` gets the **highest** budget. It is the last s
           +tep before `confirmed`, and                                  
      1110 +  every permanent failure there lands in `charged_not_booked`
           + — the one state that costs a                                
      1111 +  human. Money is already committed, so trying harder is near
           +ly free.                                                     
      1112 +- `releaseStep` gets the **lowest**. It is best-effort cleanu
           +p; the provider expires the                                  
      1113 +  hold anyway (§10.4), and a long retry loop delays the calle
           +r's `payment_failed` response                                
      1114 +  for no benefit.                                            
      1115 +- `FatalError` is thrown for anything a retry cannot change —
           + declines, unknown offers,                                   
      1116 +  illegal transitions. `RetryableError({ retryAfter })` for t
           +ransient ones. This is the                                   
      1117 +  distinction criterion #2 is asking about, and it is why the
           + classification lives in the                                 
      1118 +  step rather than in a shared retry policy.                 
      1119 +                                                             
      1120 +### 11.3 Idempotency keys                                    
      1121 +                                                             
      1122 +Per A16, only the two creates take keys:                     
      1123 +                                                             
      1124 +```                                                          
      1125 +bkg:${bookingId}:hold      → holdStep                        
      1126 +bkg:${bookingId}:charge    → chargeStep                      
      1127 +```                                                          
      1128 +                                                             
      1129 +Derived by one pure function of `(bookingId, literal step nam
           +e)` with no access to workflow                               
      1130 +or step metadata, unit-tested against a frozen table (`CORREC
           +TNESS.md` §3/L2). `consume` and                              
      1131 +`release` are keyed by `holdId`, which is the resource itself
           +.                                                            
      1132 +                                                             
      1133 +### 11.4 Validation before or after the claim                
      1134 +                                                             
      1135 +**Validate first, then claim.** A malformed body should not b
           +urn an idempotency key —                                     
      1136 +otherwise a client that fixes its payload and retries with th
           +e same key gets a permanent                                  
      1137 +`409` for a booking that never existed.                      
      1138 +                                                             
      1139 +The consequence, accepted knowingly: a rejected request's fin
           +gerprint is never stored, so a                               
      1140 +corrected retry under the same key succeeds rather than confl
           +icting. That is the right                                    
      1141 +trade — we only guard against *divergent successful* requests
           + sharing a key, which is what                                
      1142 +I5 actually protects.                                        
      1143 +                                                             
      1144 +## 12. API contracts `DECIDED`                               
      1145 +                                                             
      1146 +### 12.1 `POST /bookings`                                    
      1147 +                                                             
      1148 +```                                                          
      1149 +Headers:  Idempotency-Key: <string>        (required — A2)   
      1150 +          X-Chaos: <scenario>               (optional — A9, t
           +est/demo only)                                               
      1151 +                                                             
      1152 +Body:     { offerId: string,                                 
      1153 +            amountCents: integer > 0,                        
      1154 +            currency: string (3 letters) }                   
      1155 +```                                                          
      1156 +                                                             
      1157 +Response — a discriminated union on `state`, one arm per §10 
           +state:                                                       
      1158 +                                                             
      1159 +```jsonc                                                     
      1160 +// 201 — confirmed                                           
      1161 +{ "bookingId", "state": "confirmed", "requiresIntervention": 
           +false,                                                       
      1162 +  "holdId", "chargeId" }                                     
      1163 +                                                             
      1164 +// 202 — deadline expired, still running (A1)                
      1165 +{ "bookingId", "state": "pending" | "held" | "charged",      
      1166 +  "requiresIntervention": false,                             
      1167 +  "timelineUrl": "/bookings/{id}/timeline" }                 
      1168 +                                                             
      1169 +// 409 — inventory_unavailable                               
      1170 +// 402 — payment_failed  (+ "holdReleased": bool)            
      1171 +// 500 — charged_not_booked | payment_pending  ("requiresInte
           +rvention": true)                                             
      1172 +{ "bookingId", "state", "reason", "requiresIntervention",    
      1173 +  "holdId"?, "chargeId"? }                                   
      1174 +                                                             
      1175 +// 400 — validation failed, or Idempotency-Key missing   (no 
           +booking created)                                             
      1176 +// 409 — idempotency_key_reuse: same key, different fingerpri
           +nt (A3)                                                      
      1177 +{ "error": { "code", "message" } }                           
      1178 +```                                                          
      1179 +                                                             
      1180 +`reason` is a short human string — *"consume failed after 5 a
           +ttempts: hold expired"* — for                                
      1181 +the on-call engineer, not for programmatic branching. Branch 
           +on `state`.                                                  
      1182 +                                                             
      1183 +The `202` arm carries `timelineUrl` because that arm is preci
           +sely the case where the caller                               
      1184 +must go look somewhere else.                                 
      1185 +                                                             
      1186 +### 12.2 `GET /bookings/:id/timeline`                        
      1187 +                                                             
      1188 +`200` with the A12 envelope; `404` typed if the `bookingId` i
           +s unknown.                                                   
      1189 +                                                             
      1190 +```jsonc                                                     
      1191 +{ "booking": { "id", "idempotencyKey", "fingerprint", "state"
           +, "reason",                                                  
      1192 +               "requiresIntervention", "holdId", "chargeId", 
           +"holdReleased",                                              
      1193 +               "createdAt", "updatedAt" },                   
      1194 +  "runId": "run_...",

      1206 +## 13. Build sequence — thin verticals                       
      1207 +                                                             
      1208 +Each vertical leaves the service runnable, ends in a commit, 
           +and carries its own tests.                                   
      1209 +Build them in order; do not start one before the previous is 
           +green.                                                       
      1210 +                                                             
      1211 +| # | Vertical | Ships | Tests |                             
      1212 +|---|---|---|---|                                            
      1213 +| **V1** | **Walking skeleton** — Hono + WDK wired, one workf
           +low, `POST` → `hold` → `charge` → `consume` → `201`, all prov
           +iders scripted `ok` | The happy path, end to end | 1 happy-pa
           +th test |                                                    
      1214 +| **V2** | **Idempotency** — fingerprint, L1 claim, replay, `
           +409` on mismatch, long-poll deadline | M1, M7, I4, I5 | repla
           +y, conflict, **T-conc-1**, **T-deadline-1** |                
      1215 +| **V3** | **Failure modes** — provider scripts (`http_5xx`, 
           +`timeout`, `applied_then_lost`, `pending`, `slow`), retry cla
           +ssification, `FatalError`/`RetryableError` | M4, M5, and L2 u
           +nder retry | `applied_then_lost` on hold and on charge |     
      1216 +| **V4** | **State machine** — all eight states, `release` on
           + payment failure, `requiresIntervention`, full response union
           + | M6 — the four quadrants | one test per terminal state |   
      1217 +| **V5** | **Timeline** — the envelope, projected from the Wo
           +rld SDK | M8 | timeline after success, and after `charged_not
           +_booked` |                                                   
      1218 +| **V6** | **Docs** — `README.md`, `PROCESS.md`, known gaps |
           + M9, M10, M11 | — |                                          
      1219 +                                                             
      1220 +**V1 is deliberately first and deliberately thin.** It is the
           + vertical that touches every                                 
      1221 +unverified WDK assumption (A18, `world.runs.list()` from a te
           +st, `getRun().returnValue`                                   
      1222 +cross-request, provider singletons across the step boundary, 
           +Vitest + the bundler plugin) at                              
      1223 +the point where they are cheapest to fix. It replaces the spi
           +ke we chose to skip.                                         
      1224 +                                                             
      1225 +**Stop-and-discuss triggers.** Raise these rather than workin
           +g around them:                                               
      1226 +                                                             
      1227 +- Any WDK behaviour that contradicts D1.1 (C1–C3) or A18.    
      1228 +- `world.runs.list()` not reachable from a test process → M7'
           +s evidence changes shape.                                    
      1229 +- The Vitest/bundler integration needing more than ~15 minute
           +s.                                                           
      1230 +- Any vertical running long enough to threaten the ones after
           + it — V5 and V6 are the ones                                 
      1231 +  that get squeezed, and V6 is graded highest.               
      1232 +                                                             
      1233 +**Final pass, after V6:** exercise the service by hand with c
           +url against each failure                                     
      1234 +scenario, then add integration or unit tests for anything tha
           +t hand-testing showed the                                    
      1235 +suite does not actually cover.

❯ did you make it return a 500? id prefer a 409 or similar, so it doesnt get    
  cause in general http errors                                                  

● You're right, and it corrects a mistake in my reasoning rather than just a
  preference. I was using the HTTP status code as an alerting channel — but
  that's the wrong layer. A 5xx gets swallowed into generic server-error noise,
  trips retry middleware, and pollutes error-rate SLOs with an outcome that is
  actually the workflow working correctly. The alerting signal should be
  requiresIntervention (a flag, a metric, a queue), not the status line.

  And from the caller's side, 4xx is the more accurate instruction: don't retry 
  blindly, this needs attention.

❯ Does it simplify to drop long polling and just do fully synchronous for this  
  example project? Just conscious of time.                                      

● Yes — and more than I expected. Let me think it through rather than just
  agreeing.

  What fully-sync removes: the deadline constant and its injection, the 202 arm
  of the response union, timelineUrl, the slow(ms) script outcome, T-deadline-1,
  and the three in-flight states from the HTTP mapping entirely.

  What it appears to cost: the duplicate-request race window. Without a 202, a
  loser that finds claimed with no runId yet has no answer to give — and that
  window is exactly what T-conc-1 hits, since the winner awaits start().

  But the fix is smaller than the thing I cut earlier. The in-flight promise
  doesn't need a second Map or a synchronous-ordering rule — it's a field on the
  record:

  const r = claim(key, fp);                 // sync
  if (r.outcome === 'claimed') {
    r.record.inflight = runBooking(...);    // async fn returns a promise 
  immediately — same turn
    return await r.record.inflight;
  }
  return await r.record.inflight ?? r.record.response;   // loser

  Calling an async function returns its promise synchronously, so the assignment
  lands in the same turn as the claim. No ordering subtlety, no window.

  And it makes the concurrency test better. Under long-poll, T-conc-1's two
  requests get different responses — one 201 confirmed, one 202 pending —
  because the loser genuinely didn't know yet. Correct, but a weak demo for the
  headline requirement. Fully sync, both get byte-identical responses. That's
  the assertion the assignment is asking for.

  Net: clearly less code, a stronger test, one honest limitation for the README
  — a hung provider hangs the request; a real deployment needs the deadline and 
  a 202 path, and the timeline endpoint already supports polling, so it's a 
  small change. That's easier to defend than the long-poll was.

  Applying it.

● Committed and pushed as 55bdc57.

  Your four questions

  charged_not_booked HTTP status — went 200 → 500 → 409, and your push-back was
  the correct one. My 500 argument was using the status line as an alerting
  channel, which is the wrong layer: it trips retry middleware and buries a
  correct workflow outcome in generic server-error noise. 409 says "don't retry
  blindly, this needs attention," and requiresIntervention is the alerting
  signal.

  needs_manual_intervention — as a flag, not a state. Collapsing
  charged_not_booked and payment_pending into one state would destroy the
  diagnosis; they need different actions (refund vs. wait for the provider to
  settle). State says what happened, flag says who has to act. Ops filters on
  the flag, engineers read the state.

  Hold release — a field, not a state. payment_failed + holdReleased: false. No
  money moves, and Hold carries expiresAt so the provider reclaims it. A
  separate state would double the failure branch to distinguish two outcomes
  needing identical action. It does not set requiresIntervention — and the
  README should say that judgement depends on holds expiring in minutes, not
  hours. Honest edge noted: we cut expiry handling, so "the hold expires" is a
  property we rely on the provider for.

  Fully synchronous — yes, and it's a clear net simplification. Removes the
  deadline, the 202 arm, timelineUrl, the slow() script outcome and its test.
  The race window it seemed to cost dissolves: storing the in-flight promise on 
  the record works because calling an async function returns its promise
  synchronously. And T-conc-1 gets stronger — both requests now return
  byte-identical responses rather than one 201 and one 202.

  OPEN audit — all clear

  A13 closed by writing §11. A18 closed by deciding to build the safe way (chaos
  config as a workflow argument) and verify in V1. No decisions left.

  What got written

  §11 decomposition — four steps, one provider call each, with per-step retry
  budgets and the reasoning: consumeStep gets the highest budget (5) because
  every permanent failure there costs a human, and money is already committed;
  releaseStep gets the lowest (2) because it's best-effort cleanup and a long
  loop just delays the caller's response.

  §12 contracts, §13 the vertical build sequence, CORRECTNESS.md §5 happy path
  (13 steps, with the three that are load-bearing called out), §6 failure
  taxonomy, §7 test matrix.

  The build plan

  Six verticals, each runnable, each committed, tests alongside:

  V1 walking skeleton (happy path end-to-end) · V2 idempotency + T-conc-1 · V3
  failure modes + applied_then_lost · V4 state machine, all quadrants · V5
  timeline · V6 docs. Then hand-testing with curl, and filling test gaps that
  hand-testing exposes.

  V1 is deliberately thin and first — it touches every unverified WDK assumption
  where they're cheapest to fix, which is what replaces the spike.

  Stop-and-discuss triggers are written into §13: any WDK behaviour
  contradicting C1–C3 or A18, world.runs.list() not reachable from a test (M7's
  evidence changes shape), Vitest integration over ~15 min, or any vertical
  running long enough to squeeze V5/V6 — and V6 is graded highest.

  Ready to start V1 when you are.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Pushed as f974861. Read both documents end to end rather than trusting my edit
  history — found four real problems.

  What was actually broken

  §11.1 contradicted §11.2. §11.1 said booking state is persisted "inside the
  step that caused it," but §11.2's signatures were pure provider wrappers with
  no bookingId — so they couldn't. And the workflow body can't do I/O either, so
  nothing was writing state. Steps now take bookingId; a Map write is local and
  idempotent, so "one real side effect per step" still holds. This would have
  surfaced about ten minutes into V1.

  payment_pending strands a hold, and nothing said so. payment_failed releases;
  payment_pending must not, because the charge may still settle and releasing
  inventory we might owe the customer turns an uncertain state into a
  definitely-broken one. That asymmetry is deliberate but was entirely
  undocumented — and it's a second, better reason the state carries
  requiresIntervention. It also makes N2 the strongest of the deferred items.

  CORRECTNESS.md §5 had an ordering bug — step 4 wrote record → running(runId)
  before step 5 called start(). runId doesn't exist until start() resolves.
  Reordered, plus an explicit note that nothing may depend on runId being
  present, since that's precisely the window a duplicate can land in.

  An unexpected workflow rejection was unhandled. Not a modelled failure — a
  bug. The settled-rejected inflight promise means a retry with the same key
  gets the same 500 rather than re-running, which is right when we don't know
  what the failed run did. Now documented as the only 500 in the API.

  Plus the state diagram, which had the consume fails edge tangled into
  payment_pending's descender.

  Where the plan stands

  PLAN.md 1287 lines, CORRECTNESS.md 590. No OPEN decisions. 13 explicit cuts
  with reasons. Six verticals with stop-and-discuss triggers.

  The three things carrying the grade, in case the budget collapses:
  applied_then_lost on charge (I1 — the exercise's central case), T-conc-1 (M7,
  and the assignment names the assertion), charged-not-booked (M6's hard
  quadrant).

  My honest read going in

  The reasoning is sound and the scope is finally right — this is roughly a
  third of what it was two passes ago. The residual risk isn't design, it's that
  every WDK behaviour in here comes from documentation and I've run none of it.
  V1 exists to find that out cheaply, and four of the five unknowns have
  designed fallbacks; the fifth (world.runs.list() from a test) would change how
  M7's evidence is shaped, not whether we can produce it.

  The other thing I'd watch: planning has taken real time, and the assignment
  asks where it went. Worth logging honestly in PROCESS.md — the reversals in
  here (long-poll → sync, 500 → 409, refund promoted then demoted) are good
  annotated-prompt material precisely because they were reversals.

  Ready for V1 whenever you are.

● Concretely, with the repo currently holding nothing but markdown:

  First commit — toolchain, no booking logic

  npm init, install workflow + hono + vitest + TypeScript, wire WDK's Hono
  integration. Then the smallest possible thing that isn't the booking service
  at all: one route, one workflow, one step, one test asserting it ran.

  This is deliberately before V1's happy path. If the WDK bundler plugin and
  Vitest don't compose, every line of booking logic written first is blocked
  behind fixing it — and that's the one unknown with no designed fallback.

  While that toy workflow exists, it answers four more assumptions for
  near-free, since they're all one-liners inside a skeleton that already runs:

  - a console.log in the step body → can a step see request-scoped state? (A18)
  - a module-level counter incremented in the step, read from the test → do
  provider singletons survive the step boundary?
  - a second route calling getRun(runId).returnValue → does cross-request join
  work? (the L1 loser path)
  - world.runs.list() from the test process → does T-conc-1's assertion work at
  all? (M7)

  That's ~15 minutes and it retires the whole spike list.

  Second commit — V1 proper

  Replace the toy workflow with hold → charge → consume. Providers scripted
  all-ok. POST /bookings returns 201 confirmed. One happy-path test.

  Deliberately not in this commit: idempotency, the claim, failure modes, the
  state machine's other seven states, the timeline. Those are V2–V5.

  What I'd bring back to you

  Immediately, if any of the five assumptions comes back different —
  particularly world.runs.list(), since that reshapes how M7 gets its evidence
  rather than just costing time.

  Otherwise at the end of V1, with the happy path green and a short note on what
  WDK actually did versus what the docs implied. That note is PROCESS.md
  material either way, and it's the point where the plan's WDK claims stop being
  documentation-derived.

  One thing I'll be watching: PLAN.md §11 says every step is annotated in code
  with "what does a retry of this do in the world?". I'll write those
  annotations as I write the steps in V1, not retrofit them — they're grading
  criterion #2, and retrofitted comments read like retrofitted comments.

  Want me to start?

● Toolchain is up and pushed as 1797a71. Typecheck clean, five probe tests
  green.

  Four assumptions confirmed

  Q1 — the @workflow/vitest plugin works. This was the one risk with no
  fallback. Retired. The package ships its own docs in
  node_modules/workflow/docs, including a whole testing guide I hadn't seen from
  the website.

  Q2 — app.request() drives a workflow through Hono with no server. In-process
  runtime plus in-process HTTP. No port, no lifecycle, no supertest. The entire
  test strategy works, and D2's reasoning for Hono holds.

  Q4 — getRun(runId).returnValue resolves from a handle that didn't start the 
  run. The L1 duplicate-join path is viable.

  Q5 — world.runs.list() is reachable from the test process. T-conc-1 can assert
  I4 against the runtime's own registry, which is the strong form of M7's
  evidence.

  Also: Hono needs Nitro as its build system — but so does Express, so D2
  stands.

  One finding contradicts the plan — stop-and-discuss

  Steps execute in a separate module instance. The probe shows a step-side
  counter of 2 against a caller-side counter of 0, and the marker the HTTP
  handler wrote reads as null inside the step. Writes don't cross in either
  direction.

  This confirms A18 — and vindicates passing everything as workflow arguments.
  But it breaks PLAN.md §11.1, which I fixed only two commits ago to say
  "persisting the resulting booking state → inside the step that caused it." A
  step writing to the booking Map writes to a copy the handler and the timeline
  endpoint can never read.

  My recommendation: the workflow returns the terminal state; the handler 
  persists it.

  - Workflow body computes the state machine from step results — pure, no I/O,
  already allowed
  - Workflow returns { state, holdId, chargeId, reason, holdReleased }
  - Handler awaits run.returnValue and writes the booking record

  This is simpler than what §11.1 currently says, and it costs nothing real:
  §10.2 already establishes that in-flight states (held, charged) have no HTTP
  mapping under fully-synchronous A1, so nobody observes them anyway — and WDK's
  step log still has them for the timeline.

  The one thing it changes: if a run fails catastrophically, the booking record
  stays pending rather than reflecting how far it got. That's already the
  documented restart gap, now with a second cause.

  Providers are unaffected — they're only ever called from steps, so they share
  the step-side instance.

  Want me to apply that to PLAN.md §11 and carry on into V1's walking skeleton?

✻ Worked for 5m 45s

❯ yes apply the workflow-return/handler-persist correction. Lets extend the     
  probe to verify provider states across step routes. 1. holdStep creates a     
  hold. 2. consumeStep can read and consume that exact hold. 3. releaseStep can 
  read a hold created by holdStep. 4. retrying the same step sees the           
  provider’s prior idempotency record. 5. charge state survives between         
  separate charge-step invocations. Do not assume all steps share one provider  
  module instance merely because they are all step-side. If the probe fails,    
  stop and propose the smallest shared backing for the mock providers. Lets aim 
  to commit these probes as well as this has been apart of our planning.

  Provider probe — all five pass, no shared backing needed

  distinct step module instances: 1. All four step routes share one module
  instance, separate from the caller. Module-level Maps in the mocks are
  sufficient, given every provider access happens inside a step — which was
  already the design.

  The one I'd highlight: P4 is I1 demonstrated against the real runtime, before 
  any booking code exists. The step committed a charge, threw RetryableError,
  WDK retried it, and the retry found the committed record via the same
  idempotency key rather than charging again. That's applied_then_lost — the
  exercise's central case — working end to end. reusedExisting: true, one
  charge.

  Your instinct not to assume shared instances was the right call. If it had
  come back as 4 instances, the mocks would have needed a shared backing and §11
  would have changed a third time.

  Two consequences I recorded

  Provider state is invisible to the handler. N3 (provider-side state in the
  timeline) would need a step to read it, not a direct call from the timeline
  endpoint. It's no longer as cheap as §2.2 implies — worth knowing before
  anyone starts it.

  Provider state doesn't reset between tests in a file. The Vitest plugin clears
  workflow data per file, but module state accumulates — the probe finished
  with 12 holds after 6 runs. So every test must use distinct idempotency keys
  and offer ids, derived from the test name. That's now a rule in CORRECTNESS.md
  §4.3. It's closer to real provider behaviour than a reset hook, and avoids a
  test-only step that exists solely to clear state.

  Where V1 stands

  Toolchain up, README.md started, both probes committed as part of the planning
  record. Everything the plan asserted about WDK is now measured rather than
  inferred — and two of the assertions were wrong.

  Remaining for V1: the real POST /bookings with hold → charge → consume,
  providers scripted all-ok, returning 201 confirmed, plus its happy-path test.
  Then the probes get deleted and V2 starts on idempotency.

  Want me to carry on into the walking skeleton?

✻ Brewed for 3m 9s

❯ Any planning changes needed? or ready to continue?                            

● Three things surfaced from the probe work that the plan doesn't cover. Two are
  small; one is genuinely important.

  The important one: if a step exhausts retries and the workflow body doesn't
  catch it, run.returnValue rejects. The handler would then be unable to
  distinguish a modelled failure (payment_failed) from an actual bug — and
  CORRECTNESS.md §6.3 claims the unexpected-rejection 500 is the only 500 in the
  API. That only holds if the workflow never throws for outcomes we model.

● Three gaps recorded, pushed as b8f3788. Now ready to continue.

  What changed

  The workflow returns failures rather than throwing them (§11.3, new). The
  rule: catch every modelled failure, return a terminal record, let only genuine
  bugs propagate. Without it, a step exhausting its retries rejects
  run.returnValue, the handler can't tell payment_failed from a defect, and all
  seven failure arms of §12.1 collapse into one opaque 500. It's also the only
  place that has both the error and the context to build the response's reason.

  This one was worth catching now — it's a shape constraint on the workflow
  body, and discovering it in V4 would have meant rewriting the body after three
  verticals were built on it.

  The booking record carries runId. The timeline endpoint receives only a
  bookingId; without storing runId there's nothing to pass to
  world.events.list({ runId }). A one-field omission that would have blocked V5.

  Expected suite speed, so it doesn't get "optimised" later: ~1s per workflow
  run is the runtime's queue dispatch floor, not our code. 11 probe tests took
  17s. A ~15-test suite at 30–45s is expected.

  State of things

  No open decisions. Everything the plan asserts about WDK is now measured — and
  three assertions were wrong, all caught before code depended on them.

  Committed: 1797a71 toolchain + probe, e6b3493 provider probe + persistence
  fix, b8f3788 these gaps.

  Next: finish V1

  The walking skeleton — real POST /bookings, hold → charge → consume with
  providers scripted all-ok, returning 201 confirmed, plus a happy-path test.
  Deliberately excluded: idempotency, the claim, failure modes, the other seven
  states, the timeline.

  I'll write the per-step "what does a retry of this do in the world?"
  annotations as I write the steps, not retrofitted — they're grading criterion
  #2.

  The probes stay until the skeleton is green, then get deleted in the same
  commit that replaces them.

  Starting now unless you want to look at anything first.

● V1 done, pushed as 0b82655. Working tree clean.

  What ships

  POST /bookings → hold → charge → consume → 201 confirmed. 12 tests green (5
  unit, 7 integration), typecheck clean, and verified over real HTTP with curl:

  {"bookingId":"bkg_6fbb3945-…","state":"confirmed","requiresIntervention":false
  ,
   "holdId":"hold_6d44f069-…","chargeId":"ch_a21da0ee-…"}

  npx workflow inspect runs shows the bookingWorkflow runs, so the observability
  claim in the README is verified rather than asserted.

  One bug worth calling out

  nitro.config.ts had format: "node" — I'd copied it from the Express guide.
  Hono's default export is fetch-style, so Nitro tried to call it as a Node
  handler and every request 500'd with handler is not a function.

  The integration suite passed the whole time. app.request() drives the Hono app
  directly and bypasses Nitro entirely, so the tests were green against a
  server that couldn't serve a single request. That's a real gap in the test
  strategy — worth knowing now, and it's why PLAN.md §13's "final pass: exercise
  by hand with curl" isn't optional. I'd have shipped a broken service
  otherwise.

  Structure follows what the probes measured

  - Steps are pure provider wrappers, taking the idempotency key as an argument
  — so it lands in the step's persisted input and V5's timeline can project the
  provider call with no bespoke log.
  - The workflow returns its outcome; the handler persists it.
  - Provider state lives in module-level maps shared by all step routes,
  unreachable from the handler.

  L2 key derivation is a pure function of (bookingId, operation) with a frozen
  unit test — a change that reintroduces execution context into the key fails a
  test rather than a customer. Providers implement A16's create/transition
  split: hold/charge take keys; release/consume are keyed by resource id, no-op
  on repeat, and error on illegal transitions.

  Each step carries its "what does a retry of this do in the world?" annotation,
  written as I wrote the step.

  Honest state

  Idempotency-Key is required and stored but nothing deduplicates on it yet —
  two identical requests produce two bookings. No failure state is reachable. No
  timeline. All three are stated plainly in the README's build-progress section
  rather than left for a reader to discover.

  V2 is next: the L1 claim, replay, fingerprint conflict, and T-conc-1 — the
  test the assignment explicitly asks for.

✻ Worked for 5m 46s

❯ Any concerns? otherwise go onto V2                                            

● Two concerns, both worth acting on rather than just noting.

  1. The test suite validates a different code path than production.
  app.request() bypasses Nitro entirely — that's exactly why 12 green tests
  coexisted with a server that 500'd on every request. The vitest plugin also
  does its own directive transform, so the compilation path differs too. Manual
  curl caught it, which means §13's "final pass with curl" isn't optional
  garnish. I'll add a small scripts/smoke.sh so that check is repeatable rather
  than something I remember to do — it also serves the reviewer, since the brief
  says curl is how they'll exercise it.

  2. T-conc-1 can't assert world.runs.list().length === 1. Runs accumulate
  across tests in a worker, so it must count the delta around the concurrent
  requests. Getting this wrong would produce a test that passes for the wrong
  reason — precisely what A11 warns about.

  Building V2 with both incorporated.
